Explicit composition identities for higher composition laws
Gautam Chinta, Ajith Nair

Abstract




Abstract

In 2001, Bhargava proved a composition law for
2\times 2\times 2
integer cubes, which generalized Gauss composition of integral binary quadratic forms. Furthermore, he derived four new composition laws defined on the following spaces: 1) binary cubic forms with triplicate middle coefficients, 2) pairs of binary quadratic forms with duplicate middle coefficients, 3) pairs of quaternary alternating 2-forms and 4) senary alternating 3-forms. In each of the five cases, there is a natural group action on the underlying space with a unique polynomial invariant called the discriminant, and a notion of projectivity for the elements of the space. The strategy behind Bhargava’s approach is to construct a discriminant-preserving bijection between the set of orbits under the group action and the set of (tuples of) suitable ideal classes of quadratic rings. The projective ideal classes are equipped with a natural group structure and hence we get a group structure on the spaces of equivalence classes of projective forms of fixed discriminant
D
. In each case the class group of projective forms of discriminant
D
has a natural interpretation in terms of the narrow class group of the quadratic ring of discriminant
D
. The aim of this paper is to give explicit composition identities (similar to Gauss’ formulation of composition of binary quadratic forms) for these higher composition laws.

1.
Introduction

1.1.
Background

Let
Q_{1},Q_{2}
and
Q_{3}
be three integral binary quadratic forms of discriminant
D
. Then,
Q_{3}
is said to be a
composition
of
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
if

(1)

Q_{1}(x_{1},x_{2})Q_{2}(y_{1},y_{2})=Q_{3}(z_{1},z_{2}),

where
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
are bilinear forms in
(x_{1},x_{2})
and
(y_{1},y_{2})
, i.e.

(2)

\displaystyle z_{1}

\displaystyle=a_{11}x_{1}y_{1}+a_{12}x_{1}y_{2}+a_{21}x_{2}y_{1}+a_{22}x_{2}y_{2}

\displaystyle z_{2}

\displaystyle=b_{11}x_{1}y_{1}+b_{12}x_{1}y_{2}+b_{21}x_{2}y_{1}+b_{22}x_{2}y_{2}

for some
a_{ij},b_{ij}\in\mathbb{Z}\ (i,j=1,2)
,
and

(3)

Q_{1}(1,0)=a_{11}b_{12}-a_{12}b_{11},Q_{2}(1,0)=a_{11}b_{21}-a_{21}b_{11}.

The simplest and oldest example is the Brahmagupta-Fibonacci-Diophantus identity:

(a^{2}+b^{2})(c^{2}+d^{2})=(ac+bd)^{2}+(ad-bc)^{2},

expressing a product of a sum of two squares as again a sum of two squares.

Denote by
(\text{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{Z}^{2})^{*}
the space of binary quadratic forms with integer coefficients. The group
\text{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
acts on this space by linear substitution of the variables: a matrix
g=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in\text{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
acts on a binary quadratic form
Q(x,y)
by

(4)

Q\mapsto Q^{g}=Q(ax+by,cx+dy).

Under this action the discriminant of
Q
and the g.c.d. of its coefficients are preserved. We call the form
Q
primitive if its coefficients are relatively prime. Gauss (
[
16
]
) showed that the
\text{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
-equivalence classes of primitive binary quadratic forms of a fixed discriminant
D
have the structure of an abelian group under composition. We call this group the class group and denote it by
\text{Cl}((\text{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{Z}^{2})^{*};D)
. The identity element of the class group
\text{Cl}((\text{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{Z}^{2})^{*};D)
is the class of the principal form of discriminant
D
given by

(5)

Q_{\text{id}}(x,y)=\begin{cases}x^{2}-\frac{D}{4}y^{2}&\text{if }D\equiv 0\text{ (mod }4),\\
x^{2}+xy+\frac{1-D}{4}y^{2}&\text{if }D\equiv 1\text{ (mod }4).\end{cases}

Gauss’ formulation has the disadvantage that it is not easy to compute the composition of two given forms. Dirichlet (
[
13
]
) provides a simple method to compute the composition by taking suitable representatives equivalent to the given forms.

In 2001, Bhargava (
[
3
]
) gives the following interpretation of Gauss composition using
2\times 2\times 2
integer cubes. Consider a cube
A
(as shown below) with eight integers
a,b,c,d,e,f,g,h
at the vertices:

By slicing the cube along the three possible planes, we get three pairs of
2\times 2
matrices, say
M_{i}
and
N_{i}
(for
i=1,2,3
). Let
Q_{1}^{A},Q_{2}^{A},Q_{3}^{A}
be binary quadratic forms associated to
A
given by

(7)

Q_{i}^{A}(x,y):=-\det(M_{i}x-N_{i}y).

For example

Q_{1}^{A}(x,y):=-\det\left(\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}x-\begin{pmatrix}e&f\\
g&h\end{pmatrix}y\right),

and similarly for
Q_{2}^{A},Q_{3}^{A}
.
See Section
3
for complete definitions. The three binary quadratic forms
Q_{1}^{A},Q_{2}^{A},Q_{3}^{A}
have the same discriminant, which is also defined to be the discriminant of the cube
A
. Then, Bhargava imposes the relation on the space of primitive binary quadratic forms of a fixed discriminant
D
, that for any triple of primitive forms
Q_{1}^{A},Q_{2}^{A},Q_{3}^{A}
arising from a cube
A
, their formal sum is zero. This is the Cube Law of Bhargava
[
3
]
. This relation descends to a relation on the space of
\text{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
-classes of primitive forms of discriminant
D
, and after an identity element is chosen, this space becomes a finite abelian group. Moreover, with the choice of identity as the principal binary quadratic form of discriminant
D
given in (
5
), this group is the same as the class group
\text{Cl}((\text{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{Z}^{2})^{*};D)
.

In fact, as seen for example in Lemmermeyer (
[
21
]
, Theorem 2), the cube (
6
) also gives us the bilinear forms (
2
) in the composition identity of the forms associated to the cube. Precisely, if
Q^{A}_{1},Q^{A}_{2},Q^{A}_{3}
are the binary quadratic forms (
7
) associated to a cube
A
, then

(8)

Q^{A}_{2}(x_{2},-y_{2})Q^{A}_{3}(x_{3},-y_{3})=Q^{A}_{1}(x_{1},y_{1}),

where

x_{1}=\begin{pmatrix}x_{2}&y_{2}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}e&f\\
g&h\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}x_{3}\\
y_{3}\end{pmatrix},y_{1}=\begin{pmatrix}x_{2}&y_{2}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}x_{3}\\
y_{3}\end{pmatrix}.

The above composition identity is readily verified by substitution. (Note that the definitions of the quadratic forms
Q^{A}_{1},Q^{A}_{2},Q^{A}_{3}
are slightly different in
[
21
]
). There are two more such identities for the composition of
Q_{1}^{A}
with
Q_{2}^{A}
, and
Q_{1}^{A}
with
Q_{3}^{A}
. (See Proposition 2.2.1 in
[
22
]
.)

1.2.
The Cube Law and dual cubes

The significance of Bhargava’s approach comes not only from its reinterpretation of Gauss composition, but also from the generalizations of the notion of composition to higher degree forms. We will describe the explicit higher composition law for cubes here in the introduction and leave the detailed statements of the other cases to subsequent sections.

The space
\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, whose elements can be represented as
2\times 2\times 2
cubes as shown above, has a natural action by the group
\Gamma=\text{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})\times\text{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})\times\text{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z}).
The discriminant of the cube, (which is defined to be the common discriminant of the three binary quadratic forms associated to the cube), generates the ring of polynomial invariants of this action. A cube is said to be
projective
if the three quadratic forms associated to it are primitive.

Bhargava shows that the space of
\Gamma
-equivalence classes of projective cubes of discriminant
D
can also be equipped with the structure of a finite abelian group, which is denoted by
\text{Cl}(\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{Z}^{2};D)
. Let
[A]
denote the
\Gamma
-equivalence class of a cube
A
. The identity element in
\text{Cl}(\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{Z}^{2};D)
is
[A_{\text{id},D}]
for

(9)

A_{\text{id},D}=\hbox to91.11pt{\vbox to84.81pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.83301pt\lower-6.55522pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

as
D
is 0 or 1 mod 4.
All three quadratic forms associated to the triply symmetric cube
A_{\text{id},D}
(for
D\equiv 0\text{ or }1\text{ (mod }4)
) are equal to the principal binary quadratic form of discriminant
D
given in (
5
). When the discriminant
D
is clear from the context we write more simply
[{\mathtt{id}}]=[A_{\text{id},D}].

The group structure on the space of
\Gamma
-equivalence classes of
2\times 2\times 2
cubes of discriminant
D
follows from the following bijection:

\begin{Bmatrix}\Gamma\text{-orbits of elements of }\\
\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{Z}^{2}\end{Bmatrix}\longleftrightarrow\begin{Bmatrix}\text{isomorphism classes of pairs}\\
(S,(I_{1},I_{2},I_{3}))\end{Bmatrix}.

Here
S
is an oriented quadratic ring and
(I_{1},I_{2},I_{3})
is an equivalence class of balanced triple of oriented ideals of
S
. The discriminant of a cube equals the discriminant of the corresponding oriented quadratic ring under this bijection. See Section
3
for more details.

Alternatively, given three projective cubes
A_{1},A_{2},A_{3}
of common discriminant
D
, we have

[A_{1}]+[A_{2}]+[A_{3}]=[{\mathtt{id}}]

if and only if the sum

[Q_{i}^{A_{1}}]+[Q_{i}^{A_{2}}]+[Q_{i}^{A_{3}}]

is the identity in
\text{Cl}((\text{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{Z}^{2})^{*};D)
for each
i=1,2,3.

From the ordered triple
(A_{1},A_{2},A_{3})
of projective cubes as above we get 9 primitive quadratic forms which we arrange in a 3-by-3 matrix

\left[\begin{array}[]{ccc}Q_{1}^{A_{1}}&Q_{2}^{A_{1}}&Q_{3}^{A_{1}}\\
Q_{1}^{A_{2}}&Q_{2}^{A_{2}}&Q_{3}^{A_{2}}\\
Q_{1}^{A_{3}}&Q_{2}^{A_{3}}&Q_{3}^{A_{3}}\end{array}\right].

By the Cube Law, each row sums to the identity in the class group of binary quadratic forms. Moreover, since
[A_{1}]+[A_{2}]+[A_{3}]=[{\mathtt{id}}]
, each column sums to the identity as well. This motivates our definition of
dual cubes

(T_{1},T_{2},T_{3})
which are defined by the condition

(10)

Q_{i}^{T_{j}}=Q_{j}^{A_{i}}\mbox{\ for \ }i,j=1,2,3.

The existence of dual cubes follows from the converse part of Theorem 1 in (
[
3
]
) which states that if
[Q_{1}]+[Q_{2}]+[Q_{3}]=[{\mathtt{id}}]
in
\text{Cl}((\text{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{Z}^{2})^{*};D)
, then there is a cube giving rise to
Q_{1},Q_{2}
and
Q_{3}
.

The cube
T_{j}
is determined up to the action of
\Aut(Q_{j}^{A_{1}})\times\Aut(Q_{j}^{A_{2}})\times\Aut(Q_{j}^{A_{3}})
. It is easy to see that the dual cubes are also each of discriminant
D
and satisfy

[T_{1}]+[T_{2}]+[T_{3}]=[{\mathtt{id}}].

The dual cubes play a prominent rule in the explicit composition law for cubes as we now describe.

1.3.
Explicit composition of cubes

Before stating our first main result we need a few more definitions. Let the cube
A\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{Z}^{2}
have coefficients
(a_{ijk})
. That is,

(11)

We can think of
A
as representing either a trilinear form or a pair of bilinear forms on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
as follows:

(12)

\displaystyle A((x_{1},x_{2})^{t},

\displaystyle(y_{1},y_{2})^{t},(z_{1},z_{2})^{t})=\sum_{i,j,k=1}^{2}a_{ijk}x_{i}y_{j}z_{k}

and

(13)

\displaystyle A((y_{1},y_{2})^{t},

\displaystyle(z_{1},z_{2})^{t})=\begin{pmatrix}A(e_{1},(y_{1},y_{2})^{t},(z_{1},z_{2})^{t})\\
A(e_{2},(y_{1},y_{2})^{t},(z_{1},z_{2})^{t})\end{pmatrix}

where
e_{1},e_{2}
are the standard basis vectors.

We further define three cubes whose coefficients are expressed in terms of the coefficients of
A
. The involution
A\mapsto A^{\iota}
swaps the front and back faces

\lxSVG@closescope {

{{{{{}}{

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{79.4433pt}{17.92503pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

Next, if
Q_{1}^{A}(x,y)=px^{2}+qxy+ry^{2},
the
companion cube

A^{\prime}
of
A
is defined by

A^{\prime}(\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z})=A(\underline{x}^{\prime}-\epsilon\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z})\mbox{ for }\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2},

where
\epsilon\in\{0,1\}
is congruent to
D
mod 4 and

\underline{x}^{\prime}=\begin{pmatrix}\tfrac{q+\epsilon}{2}&-r\\
p&\tfrac{-q+\epsilon}{2}\end{pmatrix}\underline{x}.

The coefficients of the companion cube
A^{\prime}
are the
c_{ijk}
of equation (15) in
[
3
]
. See Proposition
3.3
.

Finally, define the
form product
of two cubes
A,B\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{Z}^{2}
of common discriminant
D
by

(14)

\displaystyle(A\star B)

\displaystyle(\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z};\underline{u},\underline{v},\underline{w})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle A(\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z})B^{\prime}(\underline{u},\underline{v},\underline{w})+A^{\prime}(\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z})B(\underline{u},\underline{v},\underline{w})+\epsilon A(\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z})B(\underline{u},\underline{v},\underline{w})

for
\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z},\underline{u},\underline{v},\underline{w}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}.

We can now state our first main result.

{restatable*}

[]thmcubecomp

Let
A,B,C\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{Z}^{2}
be three projective cubes of common discriminant
D
and
\epsilon\in\{0,1\}
congruent to
D
mod 4. Let
\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z},\underline{u},\underline{v},\underline{w}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}.
Then

[A]+[B]+[C]=[{\mathtt{id}}]

if and only if there exist cubes
R=(r_{ijk}),S=(s_{ijk}),T=(t_{ijk})
of discriminant
D
satisfying

(15)

\displaystyle(B\star C)

\displaystyle(\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z};\underline{u},\underline{v},\underline{w})

\displaystyle=A(R^{\sigma}(\underline{x},\underline{u}),S^{\sigma}(\underline{y},\underline{v}),T^{\sigma}(\underline{z},\underline{w}))

and

(16)

\displaystyle Q_{1}^{R}=Q_{1}^{A},\ Q_{2}^{R}=Q_{1}^{B},

and

(17)

\displaystyle Q_{1}^{B}(1,0)\cdot Q_{1}^{C}(1,0)

\displaystyle=Q_{1}^{A}(r_{211},r_{111}),

\displaystyle Q_{2}^{B}(1,0)\cdot Q_{2}^{C}(1,0)

\displaystyle=Q_{2}^{A}(s_{211},s_{111}),

\displaystyle Q_{3}^{B}(1,0)\cdot Q_{3}^{C}(1,0)

\displaystyle=Q_{3}^{A}(t_{211},t_{111}).

If this is the case, the triple of cubes
(R,S,T)
is dual to
(A,B,C)
.

We prove this result and give an example of the composition identity in Section
3
.

Remark 1.1
.

Note that we can write the composition identity in the above theorem in an alternative manner by choosing the representative

(18)

\tilde{A}=\hbox to105.56pt{\vbox to88.37pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 14.9829pt\lower-8.33301pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

in the inverse class of
[A]
. The identity we get in this case is

(B\star C)(\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z};\underline{u},\underline{v},\underline{w})=\tilde{A}(R^{\iota}(\underline{x},\underline{u}),S^{\iota}(\underline{y},\underline{v}),T^{\iota}(\underline{z},\underline{w})).

1.4.
Summary of main results and outline of paper

The full list of spaces with higher composition laws that Bhargava considers in

[
3
]
is

(1)

\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{Z}^{2}
: binary trilinear forms,

(2)

\text{Sym}^{3}\mathbb{Z}^{2}
: binary cubic forms,

(3)

\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\text{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{Z}^{2}
: pairs of binary quadratic forms,

(4)

\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\wedge^{2}\mathbb{Z}^{4}
: pairs of quaternary alternating
2
-forms, and

(5)

\wedge^{3}\mathbb{Z}^{6}
: senary alternating
3
-forms.

The spaces in (2)-(5) are obtained from the space
\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{Z}^{2}
by using certain multilinear operations, such as symmetrization, skew-symmetrization and castling.
Each of the five spaces
V(\mathbb{Z})
considered in the higher composition laws above is endowed with a natural action by a group
G(\mathbb{Z})
, and in each case, there is a unique polynomial invariant under this action, which is called the discriminant. This is in complete analogy with the case
(\text{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{Z}^{2})^{*}
of binary quadratic forms. Also, there is a notion of projectivity for elements of these spaces
V(\mathbb{Z})
which is analogous to primitivity for binary quadratic forms. Bhargava shows that in each of the five cases the set of projective
G(\mathbb{Z})
-orbits of elements in
V(\mathbb{Z})
having discriminant
D
has a natural structure of a finite abelian group.
The proof of the group structures associated to the higher composition laws relies on correspondences between the
G(\mathbb{Z})
-orbits of elements in
V(\mathbb{Z})
and certain suitable ideal classes of quadratic rings. Establishing these correspondences constitutes a major part of the work in
[
3
]
.

In this article, our goal is to formulate the five higher composition laws for the spaces above in a manner similar to Gauss’s formulation of the composition law for binary quadratic forms, without mentioning the underlying ideal class group. Theorem
1.3
above is our first example in the case of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{Z}^{2}.
The case of binary cubic forms
\text{Sym}^{3}\mathbb{Z}^{2}
has previously been considered by Nativi
[
23
]
whose results (after a simple correction) are essentially the same as ours in Section
4
.

Our results in all cases follow the pattern established in Eqs. (
1
-
3
) and Eq.
8
:

a product (or sum of products) of two forms is equated with a third form evaluated after a bilinear (or multilinear) change of variable; then the multilinear variable change is explicitly identified in terms of the two original forms.

The spaces associated to the higher composition laws also appear in the study of prehomogeneous vector spaces over algebraically closed fields by Sato-Kimura
[
18
]
, and over the field of rational numbers by Wright-Yukie
[
27
]
. Bhargava’s work illustrates that the integer orbits of these spaces also have a rich and interesting structure.

Moreover, in
[
4
]
,
[
5
]
and
[
7
]
, Bhargava describes more composition laws (also originating in the work of
[
18
]
and
[
27
]
on prehomogeneous vector spaces) where the corresponding spaces of forms parametrize arithmetic objects related to cubic, quartic and quintic rings. These parametrizations may be used to obtain results on counting number fields of degree
n\leq 5
with bounded discriminant. See, for example
[
6
,
8
]
.
There have also been some recent papers on composition identities with certain applications to Diophantine equations. Among them, we mention Choudhry
[
10
]
and Duke
[
14
]
. We also point out the thesis of M. Oller Riera
[
24
]
which builds upon the work of Bhargava in the cubic case
[
4
]
and the work of Bhargava-Ho
[
2
]
to derive composition laws of genus 1 curves.

The article is organized as follows. In Section
2
, we review the relation between Gauss composition and multiplication of ideals in quadratic rings. Sections
3
,
4
,
5
,
6
and
7
are devoted to our main task of deriving explicit composition identities for the five higher composition laws. In each of these sections, we start by explaining the correspondence between forms and ideals as described in
[
3
]
, and then go on to state and prove the composition identities, which give an alternative description of the group law on these spaces. We also include examples of composition laws at the end of the corresponding sections.

2.
Gauss composition of binary quadratic forms

As a warm-up to the identities for the higher composition laws, we quickly review the classical case of Gauss composition of binary quadratic forms. We begin by describing the correspondence between binary quadratic forms and oriented ideals using the notation in
[
3
]
.
See also
[
11
]
,
[
12
]
and
[
9
]
for further background.

A
quadratic ring
is a commutative ring (with 1) whose additive group is a free
\mathbb{Z}
-module of rank 2. Let
S
be a quadratic ring and
\langle\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\rangle
a
\mathbb{Z}
-basis of
S
. Then, the
discriminant
of
S
is defined as
\text{det}(\text{tr}(\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j}))
where
\text{tr}(x)
is the trace of the “multiplication by
x
” endomorphism. The discriminant is an integer and is congruent to
0
or
1
(mod
4)
. Let
\epsilon\in\{0,1\}
and
D\equiv\epsilon
(mod
4
). Up to isomorphism, there exists a unique quadratic ring
S(D)
of discriminant
D
with
\mathbb{Z}
-basis
\langle 1,\tau\rangle
satisfying

(19)

\tau^{2}=\epsilon\tau+\frac{D-\epsilon}{4}.

To avoid the extra automorphism of
S(D)
, which sends
\sqrt{D}
to
-\sqrt{D}
, one considers the notion of an
oriented quadratic ring
, which is a quadratic ring
S(D)
together with a choice of
\sqrt{D}
. An oriented quadratic ring is said to be
non-degenerate
if its discriminant is nonzero.

A
\mathbb{Z}
-basis
\langle 1,\tau\rangle
of
S=S(D)
is said to be positively oriented if
\text{tr}(\tau/\sqrt{D})>0
. Let
K=S\otimes\mathbb{Q}
. A
\mathbb{Z}
-basis
\langle\alpha_{1},\beta_{1},\alpha_{2},\beta_{2},...,\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}\rangle
of a rank
2n
submodule of
K^{n}
is said to be positively (or negatively) oriented if the change-of-basis matrix taking the
\mathbb{Q}
-basis

\langle(1,0,...,0),(\tau,0,...,0),(0,1,...,0),(0,\tau,...,0),...,(0,...,1),(0,...,\tau)\rangle

of
K^{n}
to the basis
\langle\alpha_{1},\beta_{1},\alpha_{2},\beta_{2},...,\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}\rangle
has positive (or negative) determinant.

An
oriented ideal
of an oriented quadratic ring
S
is a pair
(I,\mu)
, where
I
is a fractional ideal of
S
and
\mu=\pm 1
is the orientation. Multiplication of oriented ideals is defined component-wise. Two oriented ideals
(I_{1},\mu_{1})
and
(I_{2},\mu_{2})
are said to be equivalent if there exists
\kappa\in K^{\times}
such that
(I_{1},\mu_{1})=(\kappa I_{2},\text{sgn}(N(\kappa))\mu_{2})
. The norm of an oriented ideal
(I,\mu)
is given by

(20)

N((I,\mu))=\begin{vmatrix}a_{1}&a_{2}\\
b_{1}&b_{2}\end{vmatrix}

where
\langle a_{1}+a_{2}\tau,b_{1}+b_{2}\tau\rangle
is a
\mathbb{Z}
-basis of
(I,\mu)
which is oriented as per the orientation of
(I,\mu)
.
Then, the
narrow class group
of
S
, denoted by
\text{Cl}^{+}(S)
, is defined as the group of equivalence classes of invertible oriented ideals of
S
.

The next theorem provides a correspondence between binary quadratic forms and ideals in quadatic rings.

Theorem 2.1
.

Let
D
be an integer congruent to
0
or
1
modulo
4
. There is a canonical bijection between the set of
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
-classes of integral binary quadratic forms of discriminant
D
and the set of oriented ideal classes of the oriented quadratic ring
S(D)
. Under this correspondence, primitive binary quadratic forms correspond to invertible oriented ideals and the correspondence induces a bijection between the set of
\text{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
-classes of primitive binary quadratic forms of discriminant
D
and the set of invertible oriented ideal classes of the oriented quadratic ring
S(D)
.

We recall that the set of
\text{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
-equivalence classes of primitive binary quadratic forms of a fixed discriminant
D
has the structure of a finite abelian group under composition, which is called the class group and denoted by
\text{Cl}((\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{Z}^{2})^{*};D)
. The following well-known result describes the relation between the groups
\text{Cl}^{+}(S(D))
and
\text{Cl}((\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{Z}^{2})^{*};D)
.

Theorem 2.2
.

Let
D
be a nonzero integer congruent to
0
or
1
(mod
4
). There is a canonical isomorphism of groups

\mathrm{Cl}((\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{Z}^{2})^{*};D)\cong\mathrm{Cl}^{+}(S(D)).

3.
Composition of
2\times 2\times 2
cubes

As stated in Section
1
, Bhargava (
[
3
]
) presents an approach to Gauss composition using
2\times 2\times 2
cubes and also proves a composition law on the space of cubes. We briefly discuss this before describing our result on composition identities for cubes.

Let
A\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{Z}^{2}.
We represent
A
pictorially by the cube

A=\hbox to91.25pt{\vbox to86.28pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.97595pt\lower-6.80522pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{81.9433pt}{17.92503pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

with
a,b,c,d,e,f,g\in\mathbb{Z}.

For convenience we will also sometimes use the notation

A=[a,b,c,d,e,f,g,h].

The cube
A

can be sliced into three pairs of
2\times 2
matrices
(M_{i},N_{i})
, for
i=1,2,3
, given by

\begin{split}M_{1}=\begin{bmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{bmatrix},N_{1}=\begin{bmatrix}e&f\\
g&h\end{bmatrix},\\
M_{2}=\begin{bmatrix}a&e\\
b&f\end{bmatrix},N_{2}=\begin{bmatrix}c&g\\
d&h\end{bmatrix},\\
M_{3}=\begin{bmatrix}a&c\\
e&g\end{bmatrix},N_{3}=\begin{bmatrix}b&d\\
f&h\end{bmatrix}.\end{split}

Note that our
(M_{2},N_{2})
and
(M_{3},N_{3})
are swapped in comparison with
[
3
]
. We define three binary quadratic forms associated to
A
by

(21)

Q_{i}^{A}(x,y)=-\text{det}(M_{i}x-N_{i}y).

The three quadratic forms have the same discriminant, which we call also the discriminant of the cube
A
, denoted
\text{disc}(A)
. The cube
A
is called
projective
if the quadratic forms associated to
A
are all primitive, and the cube is said to be
nondegenerate
if its discriminant is nonzero.

The group
\Gamma=\text{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})\times\text{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})\times\text{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
acts on a cube
A
in the following way. The element
(\gamma,\text{id},\text{id})
acts on
A
by transforming the pair
(M_{1},N_{1})
to
(pM_{1}+qN_{1},rM_{1}+sN_{1})
, where
\gamma=\begin{bmatrix}p&q\\
r&s\end{bmatrix}
. The actions of
(\text{id},\gamma,\text{id})
and
(\text{id},\text{id},\gamma)
are defined analogously, and the three actions commute with each other, thus defining an action of the group
\Gamma
on the space of
2\times 2\times 2
cubes. Also, if
i\neq j
, then the
j^{th}
-factor of
\text{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
in
\Gamma
acts trivially on
Q_{i}^{A}
, and the
i^{th}
-factor transforms
Q_{i}^{A}(x,y)
to
Q_{i}^{A}(px-ry,-qx+sy)
. This shows that the discriminant of the cube is preserved under the action of
\Gamma
.

The Cube Law of Bhargava sets the formal sum of
Q_{1}^{A},Q_{2}^{A}
and
Q_{3}^{A}
to be trivial in the free abelian group generated by the primitive binary quadratic forms of a fixed discriminant
D=\text{disc}(A)
. The primitive binary quadratic forms that are equivalent under the action of
\text{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
get identified by the relation imposed by the Cube Law, and after choosing the identity element to be the form
Q_{\text{id,D}}
defined in (
5
), we obtain the relation of
\text{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
equivalence classes

\displaystyle[Q_{1}^{A}]+[Q_{2}^{A}]+[Q_{3}^{A}]=[Q_{\text{id,D}}].

Thus, Bhargava cubes give us Gauss composition. See equation (
8
) for an explicit composition identity involving
Q_{1}^{A},Q_{2}^{A}
and
Q_{3}^{A}
. In Section
1.2
we described how the Cube Law also gives rise to a group structure on the set of
\Gamma
-equivalence classes of projective cubes of discriminant
D
. The resulting group is denoted
\text{Cl}(\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{Z}^{2};D)
.

We will have need to introduce cubes constructed from simple (signed) permutations of the coefficients of
A
. For the cube
A=[a,b,c,d,e,f,g,h]
we define the associated cubes
A^{\iota},A^{\sigma}
and
\tilde{A}
by

\displaystyle A^{\iota}

\displaystyle=[e,f,g,h,a,b,c,d]

\displaystyle A^{\sigma}

\displaystyle=[-e,-f,-g,-h,a,b,c,d]

\displaystyle\tilde{A}

\displaystyle=[-a,b,c,-d,e,-f,-g,h].

As noted in the Introduction, if
A
is projective, then the class of
\tilde{A}
is the inverse of
[A]
.
The next proposition shows that the binary quadratic forms associated to these new cubes are simply related to
Q_{1}^{A},Q_{2}^{A},Q_{3}^{A}.

Proposition 3.1
.

Let
A
be a
2\times 2\times 2
cube. Then, the quadratic forms associated to the cubes
A^{\iota},A^{\sigma}
and
\tilde{A}
are given by

\displaystyle Q_{1}^{A^{\iota}}(x,y)

\displaystyle=Q_{1}^{A}(y,x),\ \ \ Q_{2}^{A^{\iota}}=Q_{2}^{A},Q_{3}^{A^{\iota}}=Q_{3}^{A},

\displaystyle Q_{1}^{A^{\sigma}}(x,y)

\displaystyle=Q_{1}^{A}(-y,x),\ Q_{2}^{A^{\sigma}}=Q_{2}^{A},Q_{3}^{A^{\sigma}}=Q_{3}^{A}

and

Q_{i}^{\tilde{A}}(x,y)=Q_{i}^{A}(x,-y)

for
i=1,2,3
.

3.1.
Correspondence between
2\times 2\times 2
cubes and triples of ideals

We start by recalling the correspondence between
2\times 2\times 2
cubes and
balanced
triples of oriented ideals as given in
[
3
]
. We follow the notation described in Section
2
.

Let
D
be an integer congruent to
\epsilon=0
or 1 mod 4. A triple
(I_{1},I_{2},I_{3})
of oriented ideals of
S=S(D)
is said to be
balanced
if
I_{1}I_{2}I_{3}\subset S
and
N(I_{1})N(I_{2})N(I_{3})=1.

Two balanced triples
(I_{1},I_{2},I_{3})
and
(J_{1},J_{2},J_{3})
are said to be
equivalent
if there exist
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3}\in K^{\times}
such that
I_{m}=\lambda_{m}J_{m}
for
m=1,2,3
.

By Theorem 11 of
[
3
]
, there is a bijection between the nondegenerate
\Gamma
-orbits of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{Z}^{2}
and the set of isomorphism classes of pairs
(S,(I_{1},I_{2},I_{3}))
, where
S
is a nondegenerate oriented quadratic ring and
(I_{1},I_{2},I_{3})
is an equivalence class of balanced triple of oriented ideals of
S
. Under this bijection, the discriminant of a cube equals the discriminant of the corresponding oriented quadratic ring. The correspondence is obtained as follows.

Suppose we are given a pair
(S,(I_{1},I_{2},I_{3}))
as described above. Let
\langle 1,\tau\rangle
be the positively oriented
\mathbb{Z}
-basis of
S
, where
\tau
satisfies the equation (
19
). Let
\langle\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\rangle,\langle\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\rangle
and
\langle\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\rangle
be
\mathbb{Z}
-bases for
I_{1},I_{2}
and
I_{3}
respectively, which are oriented according to the respective orientation of the ideals. The balanced condition then implies that there exist 16 integers
a_{ijk}
and
a^{\prime}_{ijk}
for
i,j,k\in\{1,2\}
such that

(22)

\alpha_{i}\beta_{j}\gamma_{k}=a^{\prime}_{ijk}+a_{ijk}\tau.

The cube corresponding to
(S,(I_{1},I_{2},I_{3}))
is
A=\{a_{ijk}\}
. To go from a cube
A=\{a_{ijk}\}
to a pair
(S,(I_{1},I_{2},I_{3}))
, we first note that the ring
S
is determined by its discriminant which equals the discriminant of the cube. Then, we solve the system of equations given by (
22
) for
\alpha_{i}
’s,
\beta_{j}
’s and
\gamma_{k}
’s. Here, the values of
a^{\prime}_{ijk}
’s are uniquely determined in terms of the coefficients
a_{ijk}
as given in (
[
3
]
). The corresponding triple of oriented ideals is determined uniquely up to equivalence. Indeed, we can choose the triple of ideals with
\mathbb{Z}
-bases given by the following.

(23)

\begin{split}\alpha_{1}&=a^{\prime}_{111}+a_{111}\tau,\alpha_{2}=a^{\prime}_{211}+a_{211}\tau,\\
\beta_{1}&=a^{\prime}_{212}+a_{212}\tau,\beta_{2}=a^{\prime}_{222}+a_{222}\tau,\\
\gamma_{1}&=1/\beta_{1},\gamma_{2}=1/\alpha_{2}.\end{split}

We call a choice of ordered
\mathbb{Z}
-bases for
I_{1},I_{2},I_{3}

compatible
with the cube
A
if the equations (
22
) hold. Henceforth whenever we introduce a cube with associated pair
(S,(I_{1},I_{2},I_{3}))
we always assume the bases chosen for the
I_{j}
are compatible in this sense.

The
S
-module structure of the ideals can be described in terms of the cube:

Proposition 3.2

(
[
3
]
, Eq. (20))
.

The
S
-module structure of
I_{1}
is given by

(24)

\begin{split}\tau\alpha_{1}&=\frac{q_{1}+\epsilon}{2}\alpha_{1}+p_{1}\alpha_{2},\\
\tau\alpha_{2}&=-r_{1}\alpha_{1}+\frac{\epsilon-q_{1}}{2}\alpha_{2},\end{split}

where
Q_{1}^{A}(x,y)=p_{1}x^{2}+q_{1}xy+r_{1}y^{2}
, and
\epsilon\in\{0,1\}
is congruent to
D
(mod
4
). The
S
-module structures of
I_{2}
and
I_{3}
are similarly given in terms of
Q_{2}^{A}
and
Q_{3}^{A}
.

Under the correspondence between cubes and balanced triples of ideals, the projective orbits of
2\times 2\times 2
cubes of discriminant
D
correspond to balanced triples of projective ideals of
S(D)
. Also, the set of equivalence classes of balanced triples of projective oriented ideals has a natural structure of an abelian group under component-wise multiplication i.e. the product of the balanced triples
(I_{1},I_{2},I_{3})
and
(J_{1},J_{2},J_{3})
is
(I_{1}J_{1},I_{2}J_{2},I_{3}J_{3})
. Thus, the set of projective orbits of
2\times 2\times 2
cubes of discriminant
D
gets an abelian group structure, which is denoted as
\text{Cl}(\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{Z}^{2};D)
. We also have the following group isomorphism:

\mathrm{Cl}(\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{Z}^{2};D)\cong\mathrm{Cl}^{+}(S(D))\times\mathrm{Cl}^{+}(S(D)).

This is obtained by sending a projective balanced triple
(I_{1},I_{2},I_{3})
to
(I_{1},I_{2})
, because the third ideal is determined from the first two.

In the next three propositions we continue to let
A=(a_{ijk})
be a cube corresponding to the balanced triple of oriented ideals
(I_{1},I_{2},I_{3}).
We fix compatible bases
\langle\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\rangle,\langle\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\rangle
and
\langle\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\rangle
for the three ideals, respectively.

Recall that the companion cube
A^{\prime}
of a cube
A
was defined in the introduction to be

(25)

A^{\prime}(\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z})=A(\underline{x}^{\prime}-\epsilon\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z})\mbox{ for }\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2},

for

\underline{x}^{\prime}=\begin{pmatrix}\tfrac{q+\epsilon}{2}&-r\\
p&\tfrac{-q+\epsilon}{2}\end{pmatrix}\underline{x}.

The next proposition identifies the coefficients of the companion cube with the
a^{{}^{\prime}}_{ijk}
defined in (
22
). In
[
3
]
by comparison, Bhargava shows that the coefficients
a^{\prime}_{ijk}
(which he denotes by
c_{ijk}
in his Eq. (15)) are determined by the cube
A
by giving the unique explicit solution to the system of 18 linear and quadratic equations arising from the associativity and commutativity of multiplication in the ring
S
.

Proposition 3.3
.

Let
A
be a
2\times 2\times 2
cube.
Then, the companion cube
A^{\prime}
has coefficients equal to
a^{\prime}_{ijk}
.

Proof.

By (
22
), for
\underline{x}=(x_{1},x_{2})^{t},\underline{y}=(y_{1},y_{2})^{t},\underline{z}=(z_{1},z_{2})^{t}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, the cube
A
defines a trilinear form
C
satisfying

(26)

(x_{1}\alpha_{1}+x_{2}\alpha_{2})(y_{1}\beta_{1}+y_{2}\beta_{2})(z_{1}\gamma_{1}+z_{2}\gamma_{2})=A(\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z})\tau+C(\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z}).

Let
a^{\prime}_{ijk}
be the coefficients of the cube
C.
Our goal is to show that
A^{\prime}=C.

Multiplying both sides of equation (
26
) by
\tau
,

\tau(x_{1}\alpha_{1}+x_{2}\alpha_{2})(y_{1}\beta_{1}+y_{2}\beta_{2})(z_{1}\gamma_{1}+z_{2}\gamma_{2})=A(\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z})\tau^{2}+C(\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z})\tau.

Set

(27)

\underline{x}^{\prime}=\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}^{\prime}\\
x_{2}^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{q_{1}+\epsilon}{2}&-r_{1}\\
p_{1}&\frac{-q_{1}+\epsilon}{2}\end{pmatrix}\underline{x},

so that
\tau(x_{1}\alpha_{1}+x_{2}\alpha_{2})=(x^{\prime}_{1}\alpha_{1}+x^{\prime}_{2}\alpha_{2})
by Proposition
3.2
.
Consequently

A(\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z})\tau^{2}+C(\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z})\tau=A(\underline{x}^{\prime},\underline{y},\underline{z})\tau+C(\underline{x}^{\prime},\underline{y},\underline{z}).

Replace
\tau^{2}
by
\epsilon\tau+\left(\frac{D-\epsilon}{4}\right)
and compare coefficients of
\tau
on both sides. This yields

\begin{split}C(\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z})&=A(\underline{x}^{\prime},\underline{y},\underline{z})-\epsilon A(\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z})\\
&=A(\underline{x}^{\prime}-\epsilon\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z})\\
&=A^{\prime}(\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z}).\end{split}

This proves that the coefficients of the companion cube
A^{\prime}
are equal to
a_{ijk}^{\prime}
.
∎

Define the matrix

L_{i}^{A}=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{q_{i}-\epsilon}{2}&-r_{i}\\
p_{i}&\frac{-q_{i}-\epsilon}{2}\end{pmatrix},

where
Q_{i}^{A}(x,y)=p_{i}x^{2}+q_{i}xy+r_{i}y^{2}
. Arguing as in the previous proof, we see that

(28)

A^{\prime}(\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z})=A(L_{1}^{A}\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z})=A(\underline{x},L_{2}^{A}\underline{y},\underline{z}),A(\underline{x},\underline{y},L_{3}^{A}\underline{z}).

The next proposition gives the relation between the quadratic forms associated to a cube
A
and a triple of ideals corresponding to it.

Proposition 3.4
.

With the choice of the
\mathbb{Z}
-bases of the ideals
I_{1},I_{2},I_{3}
as given in equation (
23
), we have

(29)

\begin{split}N(x\alpha_{1}-y\alpha_{2})/N(I_{1})=Q_{1}^{A}(x,y),\\
N(x\beta_{1}-y\beta_{2})/N(I_{2})=Q_{2}^{A}(x,y),\\
N(x\gamma_{1}-y\gamma_{2})/N(I_{3})=Q_{3}^{A}(x,y).\end{split}

Proof.

This is a straightforward computation using Proposition
3.2
and the formula for the
a^{\prime}_{ijk}
’s given on page 235 of
[
3
]
.
∎

Recall that
\alpha_{i}\beta_{j}\gamma_{k}=a_{ijk}\tau+a^{\prime}_{ijk}.
Our final result of this subsection gives a formula for the products
\beta_{j}\gamma_{k}.

Proposition 3.5
.

For
j,k\in\{1,2\}
,

N(I_{1})\beta_{j}\gamma_{k}=(a_{2jk})\overline{\alpha_{1}}+(a_{1jk})(-\overline{\alpha_{2}}).

Equivalently, we have

N(I_{1})(y_{1}\beta_{1}+y_{2}\beta_{2})(z_{1}\gamma_{1}+z_{2}\gamma_{2})=A(\underline{e}_{2},\underline{y},\underline{z})\overline{\alpha_{1}}+A(\underline{e}_{1},\underline{y},\underline{z})(-\overline{\alpha_{2}})

for
\underline{y}=(y_{1},y_{2})^{t},\underline{z}=(z_{1},z_{2})^{t}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}.

Proof.

Multiplying Eq. (
22
) by
\overline{\alpha_{1}}
on both sides gives

N(\alpha_{1})\beta_{j}\gamma_{k}=a^{\prime}_{1jk}\overline{\alpha_{1}}+a_{1jk}\tau\overline{\alpha_{1}}.

Take conjugates on both sides of Eq. (
24
) and simplify to get an expression for
\tau\overline{\alpha_{1}}
. Substituting that expression in the equation above, we get

\begin{split}N(\alpha_{1})\beta_{j}\gamma_{k}&=a^{\prime}_{1jk}\overline{\alpha_{1}}+a_{1jk}\left(-\left(\frac{q_{1}-\epsilon}{2}\right)\overline{\alpha_{1}}-p_{1}\overline{\alpha_{2}}\right)\\
&=\left(a^{\prime}_{1jk}-a_{1jk}\left(\frac{q_{1}-\epsilon}{2}\right)\right)\overline{\alpha_{1}}+(a_{1jk}p_{1})(-\overline{\alpha_{2}}).\end{split}

Also,
\frac{N(\alpha_{1})}{N(I_{1})}=p_{1}
by Prop.
3.4
. So we obtain

p_{1}N(I_{1})\beta_{j}\gamma_{k}=\left(a^{\prime}_{1jk}-a_{1jk}\left(\frac{q_{1}-\epsilon}{2}\right)\right)\overline{\alpha_{1}}+(a_{1jk}p_{1})(-\overline{\alpha_{2}}).

Similarly, multiplying Eq. (
22
) by
\overline{\alpha_{2}}
on both sides and arguing as above, we get

r_{1}N(I_{1})\beta_{j}\gamma_{k}=(a_{2jk}r_{1})\overline{\alpha_{1}}+\left(-a^{\prime}_{2jk}-a_{2jk}\left(\frac{q_{1}+\epsilon}{2}\right)\right)(-\overline{\alpha_{2}}).

Finally, comparing the last two equations yields

N(I_{1})\beta_{j}\gamma_{k}=(a_{2jk})\overline{\alpha_{1}}+(a_{1jk})(-\overline{\alpha_{2}}).

∎

3.2.
Composition identities for
2\times 2\times 2
cubes

We restate our main result on composition identities for
2\times 2\times 2
cubes from the introduction.

\cubecomp

3.3.
Proof of Theorem
1.3
, forward direction

Proof.

Assume that

[A]+[B]+[C]=[{\mathtt{id}}].

We choose the balanced triples
(I_{1},I_{2},I_{3}),(J_{1},J_{2},J_{3}),(K_{1},K_{2},K_{3})
corresponding to
A,B
and
C
so that
I_{m}J_{m}K_{m}=S
for
m=1,2,3
. Indeed, since the triples
(I_{1}J_{1}K_{1},I_{2}J_{2}K_{2},I_{3}J_{3}K_{3})
and
(S,S,S)
are equivalent, we can rescale to assume that
I_{m}J_{m}K_{m}=S
for each
m.

We choose compatible bases

•

\langle\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\rangle,\langle\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\rangle,\langle\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\rangle
for
I_{1},I_{2},I_{3}

•

\langle\omega_{1},\omega_{2}\rangle
,
\langle\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\rangle
,
\langle\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\rangle
for
J_{1},J_{2},J_{3}

•

\langle\pi_{1},\pi_{2}\rangle,\langle\chi_{1},\chi_{2}\rangle,\langle\psi_{1},\psi_{2}\rangle
for
K_{1},K_{2},K_{3}.

Let
R=(r_{ijk}),S=(s_{ijk})
and
T=(t_{ijk})
be cubes corresponding to the balanced triples
(I_{1},J_{1},K_{1}),(I_{2},J_{2},K_{2})
and
(I_{3},J_{3},K_{3})
, with the same choices for bases of the ideals. It follows from Proposition
3.4
that the cubes
(R,S,T)
are dual to the cubes
(A,B,C)
.
We note that the cube
\tilde{A}

defined in (
18
) corresponds to the oriented triple
(I_{1}^{-1},I_{2}^{-1},I_{3}^{-1})
with compatible bases

\langle\alpha_{1}^{\prime},-\alpha_{2}^{\prime}\rangle,\langle\beta_{1}^{\prime},-\beta_{2}^{\prime}\rangle,\langle\gamma_{1}^{\prime},-\gamma_{2}^{\prime}\rangle

where
\alpha_{i}^{\prime}=N(I_{1})^{-1}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{\alpha}_{i},\beta_{i}^{\prime}=N(I_{2})^{-1}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{\beta}_{i},\gamma_{i}^{\prime}=N(I_{3})^{-1}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{\gamma}_{i}
for
i=1,2.

Applying Proposition
3.5
to the cubes
R,S
and
T
, we get that

(30)

\begin{split}(x_{1}\omega_{1}+x_{2}\omega_{2})(u_{1}\pi_{1}+u_{2}\pi_{2})=a_{1}\alpha_{1}^{\prime}+a_{2}\alpha_{2}^{\prime},\\
(y_{1}\sigma_{1}+y_{2}\sigma_{2})(v_{1}\chi_{1}+v_{2}\chi_{2})=b_{1}\beta_{1}^{\prime}+b_{2}\beta_{2}^{\prime},\\
(z_{1}\theta_{1}+z_{2}\theta_{2})(w_{1}\psi_{1}+w_{2}\psi_{2})=c_{1}\gamma_{1}^{\prime}+c_{2}\gamma_{2}^{\prime},\end{split}

where
\underline{a}=(a_{1},a_{2})^{t}=R^{\iota}(\underline{x},\underline{u}),\underline{b}=(b_{1},b_{2})^{t}=S^{\iota}(\underline{y},\underline{v}),\underline{c}=(c_{1},c_{2})^{t}=T^{\iota}(\underline{z},\underline{w})
.

Multiplying together the equations in (
30
), yields the identity

(31)

[B(\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z})\tau+B^{\prime}(\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z})][C(\underline{u},\underline{v},\underline{w})\tau+C^{\prime}(\underline{u},\underline{v},\underline{w})]=\tilde{A}(\underline{a},\underline{b},\underline{c})\tau+\tilde{A}^{\prime}(\underline{a},\underline{b},\underline{c}).

The cube
\tilde{A}
is a representative of the inverse class of the cube
A
and has the property that

(32)

\tilde{A}((a_{1},a_{2})^{t},(b_{1},b_{2})^{t},(c_{1},c_{2})^{t})=A((-a_{1},a_{2})^{t},(-b_{1},b_{2})^{t},(-c_{1},c_{2})^{t}).

Substituting this relation in (
31
), expanding and equating the coefficients of
\tau
on both sides gives us

(B\star C)(\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z};\underline{u},\underline{v},\underline{w})=A(R^{\sigma}(\underline{x},\underline{u}),S^{\sigma}(\underline{y},\underline{v}),T^{\sigma}(\underline{z},\underline{w})).

Lastly, we obtain

\displaystyle Q_{1}^{B}(1,0)\cdot Q_{1}^{C}(1,0)

\displaystyle=Q_{1}^{A}(r_{211},r_{111}),

\displaystyle Q_{2}^{B}(1,0)\cdot Q_{2}^{C}(1,0)

\displaystyle=Q_{2}^{A}(s_{211},s_{111}),

\displaystyle Q_{3}^{B}(1,0)\cdot Q_{3}^{C}(1,0)

\displaystyle=Q_{3}^{A}(t_{211},t_{111}).

by applying the equation (
8
) to the cubes
R,S,T
respectively.

3.4.
Proof of Theorem
1.3
, reverse direction.

Now suppose
A,B,C
are three projective cubes of common discriminant
D\equiv\epsilon
mod 4. Let
\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z},\underline{u},\underline{v},\underline{w}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}.
Suppose there exist cubes
R=(r_{ijk}),S=(s_{ijk}),T=(t_{ijk})
of discriminant
D
satisfying the identity

\displaystyle(B\star C)(\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z};\underline{u},\underline{v},\underline{w})=A(R^{\sigma}(\underline{x},\underline{u}),S^{\sigma}(\underline{y},\underline{v}),T^{\sigma}(\underline{z},\underline{w})),

for all
\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z},\underline{u},\underline{v},\underline{w}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
.
We also assume (
16
) and (
17
).
Using equation (
32
), we have that

\displaystyle(B\star C)

\displaystyle(\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z};\underline{u},\underline{v},\underline{w})=\tilde{A}(R^{\iota}(\underline{x},\underline{u}),S^{\iota}(\underline{y},\underline{v}),T^{\iota}(\underline{z},\underline{w})).

We will first show that the above identity implies that

(33)

\displaystyle[B(\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z})\tau

\displaystyle+B^{\prime}(\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z})][C(\underline{u},\underline{v},\underline{w})\tau+C^{\prime}(\underline{u},\underline{v},\underline{w})]

\displaystyle=\tilde{A}(R^{\iota}(\underline{x},\underline{u}),S^{\iota}(\underline{y},\underline{v}),T^{\iota}(\underline{z},\underline{w})\tau+\tilde{A}^{\prime}(R^{\iota}(\underline{x},\underline{u}),S^{\iota}(\underline{y},\underline{v}),T^{\iota}(\underline{z},\underline{w})).

Expanding out equation (
33
), we see that it suffices to show

(34)

\displaystyle B^{\prime}(\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z})C^{\prime}(\underline{u},\underline{v},\underline{w})+\frac{(D-\epsilon)}{4}

\displaystyle B(\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z})C(\underline{u},\underline{v},\underline{w})

\displaystyle=\tilde{A}^{\prime}(R^{\iota}(\underline{x},\underline{u}),S^{\iota}(\underline{y},\underline{v}),T^{\iota}(\underline{z},\underline{w})).

Before proceeding, we explicate the group action on cubes when the cube is thought of as a pair of bilinear forms.
For
\gamma
a two-by-two matrix, the action takes the form

(35)

\displaystyle((\gamma,{\mathtt{id}},{\mathtt{id}})\circ R)(\underline{x},\underline{u})

\displaystyle=\gamma(R(\underline{e}_{1},\underline{x},\underline{u}),R(\underline{e}_{2},\underline{x},\underline{u}))^{t}

\displaystyle(({\mathtt{id}},\gamma,{\mathtt{id}})\circ R)(\underline{x},\underline{u})

\displaystyle=R(\gamma^{t}\underline{x},\underline{u})

\displaystyle(({\mathtt{id}},{\mathtt{id}},\gamma)\circ R)(\underline{x},\underline{u})

\displaystyle=R(\underline{x},\gamma^{t}\underline{u}).

By the definition of companion cube given in (
25
), we have

\displaystyle\tilde{A}^{\prime}(R^{\iota}(\underline{x},\underline{u}),S^{\iota}(\underline{y},\underline{v}),T^{\iota}(\underline{z},\underline{w}))=\tilde{A}(L_{1}^{\tilde{A}}R^{\iota}(\underline{x},\underline{u}),S^{\iota}(\underline{y},\underline{v}),T^{\iota}(\underline{z},\underline{w})).

The first argument of
\tilde{A}
is

(36)

L_{1}^{\tilde{A}}R^{\iota}(\underline{x},\underline{u})=L_{1}^{\tilde{A}}\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}R(\underline{x},\underline{u})=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}(L_{1}^{A})^{t}R(\underline{x},\underline{u}).

Using the description of the group action from (
35
), the last expression becomes

\displaystyle\left(\left(\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}(L_{1}^{A})^{t},{\mathtt{id}},{\mathtt{id}}\right)\circ R\right)

\displaystyle(\underline{x},\underline{u})=\left(\left(\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}(L_{1}^{R})^{t},{\mathtt{id}},{\mathtt{id}}\right)\circ R\right)(\underline{x},\underline{u}),\mbox{ by (\ref{eq:cubecompQ})}

\displaystyle=\left(\left(\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix},(L_{2}^{R})^{t},{\mathtt{id}}\right)\circ R\right)(\underline{x},\underline{u}),\mbox{ by (\ref{eq:companionL})}

\displaystyle=\left(\left(\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix},{\mathtt{id}},{\mathtt{id}}\right)\circ R\right)(L_{2}^{R}\underline{x},\underline{u})

\displaystyle=R^{\iota}(L_{2}^{R}\underline{x},\underline{u}).

Hence,

\displaystyle\tilde{A}^{\prime}

\displaystyle(R^{\iota}(\underline{x},\underline{u}),S^{\iota}(\underline{y},\underline{v}),T^{\iota}(\underline{z},\underline{w}))

\displaystyle=\tilde{A}(R^{\iota}(L_{2}^{R}\underline{x},\underline{u}),S^{\iota}(\underline{y},\underline{v}),T^{\iota}(\underline{z},\underline{w}))

\displaystyle=(B\star C)(L_{2}^{R}\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z};\underline{u},\underline{v},\underline{w})

\displaystyle=B(L_{2}^{R}\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z})C^{\prime}(\underline{u},\underline{v},\underline{w})+B^{\prime}(L_{2}^{R}\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z})C(\underline{u},\underline{v},\underline{w})+\epsilon B(L_{2}^{R}\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z})C(\underline{u},\underline{v},\underline{w})

\displaystyle=B^{\prime}(\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z})C^{\prime}(\underline{u},\underline{v},\underline{w})+B^{\prime}(L_{1}^{B}\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z})C(\underline{u},\underline{v},\underline{w})+\epsilon B^{\prime}(\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z})C(\underline{u},\underline{v},\underline{w}),

since
L_{2}^{R}=L_{1}^{B}
. Lastly, it is easy to check that
B^{\prime}(L_{1}^{B}\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z})=\left(\frac{D-\epsilon}{4}\right)B(\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z})
, thus establishing equations (
34
) and (
33
).

Now, we proceed to show how equation (
33
) implies that
[A]+[B]+[C]=[\text{id}]
, or equivalently,
[B]+[C]=[\tilde{A}]
. We continue to use the notation from the start of Section
3.3
for the balanced triples and compatible bases corresponding to
A,B,C
and
\tilde{A}.

Using equation (
33
), we get that

(x_{1}\omega_{1}+x_{2}\omega_{2})(y_{1}\sigma_{1}+y_{2}\sigma_{2})(z_{1}\theta_{1}+z_{2}\theta_{2})(u_{1}\pi_{1}+u_{2}\pi_{2})\\
(v_{1}\chi_{1}+v_{2}\chi_{2})(w_{1}\psi_{1}+w_{2}\psi_{2})=(a_{1}\alpha_{1}^{\prime}+a_{2}\alpha_{2}^{\prime})(b_{1}\beta_{1}^{\prime}+b_{2}\beta_{2}^{\prime})(c_{1}\gamma_{1}^{\prime}+c_{2}\gamma_{2}^{\prime}),

where
(a_{1},a_{2})^{t}=R^{\iota}(\underline{x},\underline{u}),(b_{1},b_{2})^{t}=S^{\iota}(\underline{y},\underline{v}),(c_{1},c_{2})^{t}=T^{\iota}(\underline{z},\underline{w}).
We substitute
y_{1}=z_{1}=1,y_{2}=z_{2}=0,v_{1}=w_{1}=1
and
v_{2}=w_{2}=0
in the above equation to get

(x_{1}\omega_{1}+x_{2}\omega_{2})\sigma_{1}\theta_{1}(u_{1}\pi_{1}+u_{2}\pi_{2})\chi_{1}\psi_{1}\\
=(a_{1}\alpha_{1}^{\prime}+a_{2}\alpha_{2}^{\prime})(s_{211}\beta_{1}^{\prime}+s_{111}\beta_{2}^{\prime})(t_{211}\gamma_{1}^{\prime}+t_{111}\gamma_{2}^{\prime}).

This shows that
J_{1}K_{1}\subset\alpha I_{1}^{-1}
for some
\alpha\in K
. If
N(J_{1}K_{1})=N(\alpha)N(I_{1}^{-1})
, then
J_{1}K_{1}
and
I_{1}^{-1}
are equivalent (oriented) ideals. Therefore we need to show that

N(J_{1})N(K_{1})N(\sigma_{1}\theta_{1}\chi_{1}\psi_{1})=N(I_{1}^{-1})N((s_{211}\beta_{1}^{\prime}+s_{111}\beta_{2}^{\prime})(t_{211}\gamma_{1}^{\prime}+t_{111}\gamma_{2}^{\prime})).

By Proposition
3.4
, the previous equality is equivalent to

N(J_{1})N(K_{1})Q_{2}^{B}(1,0)N(J_{2})Q_{3}^{B}(1,0)N(J_{3})Q_{2}^{C}(1,0)N(K_{2})Q_{3}^{C}(1,0)N(K_{3})\\
=N(I_{1}^{-1})N(I_{2}^{-1})Q_{2}^{\tilde{A}}(s_{211},-s_{111})N(I_{3}^{-1})Q_{3}^{\tilde{A}}(t_{211},-t_{111}).

The above equation follows from the second and third equalities in (
17
), and hence
J_{1}K_{1}=I_{1}^{-1}
. Similarly, we deduce that
J_{2}K_{2}=I_{2}^{-1}
and
J_{3}K_{3}=I_{3}^{-1}
. We conclude that
[B]+[C]=[\tilde{A}]
, thus completing the proof of the theorem.

∎

Example

We illustrate the composition identity of
2\times 2\times 2
cubes with an example.

A,B
and
C
shown below.

The cubes are of discriminant
D=-47
and they satisfy

[A]+[B]+[C]=[\text{id}]

in
\text{Cl}(\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{Z}^{2};-47).
Also, the associated quadratic forms are given by
Q_{1}^{A}=Q_{1}^{B}=[2,1,6],Q_{1}^{C}=[4,-1,3],Q_{2}^{A}=[1,-1,12],Q_{2}^{B}=[1,1,12],Q_{2}^{C}=[1,1,12],Q_{3}^{A}=Q_{3}^{B}=[2,-1,6]
and
Q_{3}^{C}=[4,1,3].

are dual to the cubes
(A,B,C)
. The cubes
A,B,C
satisfy the composition identity

\displaystyle(B\star C)

\displaystyle(\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z};\underline{u},\underline{v},\underline{w})

\displaystyle=A(R^{\sigma}(\underline{x},\underline{u}),S^{\sigma}(\underline{y},\underline{v}),T^{\sigma}(\underline{z},\underline{w}))

and

\displaystyle Q_{1}^{B}(1,0)\cdot Q_{1}^{C}(1,0)

\displaystyle=Q_{1}^{A}(r_{211},r_{111})

\displaystyle Q_{2}^{B}(1,0)\cdot Q_{2}^{C}(1,0)

\displaystyle=Q_{2}^{A}(s_{211},s_{111})

\displaystyle Q_{3}^{B}(1,0)\cdot Q_{3}^{C}(1,0)

\displaystyle=Q_{3}^{A}(t_{211},t_{111}).

4.
Composition of binary cubic forms

We next consider the space
\mathrm{Sym}^{3}\mathbb{Z}^{2}
of integral binary cubic forms with triplicate central coefficients, that is, polynomials
f(x,y)
of the form
a_{0}x^{3}+3a_{1}x^{2}y+3a_{2}xy^{2}+a_{3}y^{3}
. Such a binary cubic form has a natural representation as the triply symmetric cube

(37)

Indeed the polarization formula gives a bijective correspondence between binary cubic forms on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
and symmetric trilinear forms on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}\times\mathbb{Z}^{2}\times\mathbb{Z}^{2}.

The main result of this section, Theorem
4.2
, is an explicit composition formula for binary cubic forms. Such a result has previously been established by B. Nativi (
[
23
]
). Under the hood, our methods are essentially the same as his, however, using our results from the previous section, we are able to give a more streamlined proof. See Remark
4.3
for a further comparison with Nativi’s work.

We briefly mention some other related works on composition laws for binary cubic forms. The work of Slupinski-Stanton (
[
26
]
) provides a detailed analysis of the structure of binary cubic forms (over any field with characteristic not equal to 2 or 3) from the perspective of symplectic geometry, and also describes the group structure on the classes of binary cubic forms. (See Theorems 3.34 and 3.46 in
[
26
]
.) This is investigated more closely in the PhD thesis of G. Yassiyevich (
[
28
]
) which describes the relation between the class groups in the integral and rational cases.

Classically, binary cubic forms have also been investigated from the perspective of invariant theory, for example, by Cayley and Eisenstein. In fact, the syzygy we obtain in Section
4.3
was proved by Eisenstein (
[
15
]
). There is also an approach to Gauss composition of binary quadratic forms using Clifford algebras, due to M. Kneser (
[
19
]
), which was used by Hoffman-Morales (
[
17
]
) to generalize Eisenstein’s theory of binary cubic forms to binary cubic forms over arbitrary integral domains with characteristic not equal to 2 or 3.

4.1.
The group law for binary cubic forms

We follow the notation and definitions from Section
2
.

Let
f(x,y)=a_{0}x^{3}+3a_{1}x^{2}y+3a_{2}xy^{2}+a_{3}y^{3}
be a binary cubic form associated to the triply-symmetric cube
A_{f}
as in (
37
). If
A_{f}
is projective then
f
is also called projective. The discriminant of
f(x,y)
is defined to be the discriminant of the corresponding cube
A_{f}
. Explicitly, it is given by

\text{disc}(f)=-3a_{1}^{2}a_{2}^{2}+4a_{0}a_{2}^{3}+4a_{1}^{3}a_{3}-6a_{0}a_{1}a_{2}a_{3}+a_{0}^{2}a_{3}^{2}.

We say that
f(x,y)
is nondegenerate if its discriminant is nonzero.

In
[
3
]
, Bhargava constructs a canonical bijection between the set of nondegenerate
\text{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
-orbits of
\text{Sym}^{3}\mathbb{Z}^{2}
and the set of equivalence classes of triples
(S,I,\delta)
, where
S
is a nondegenerate oriented quadratic ring,
I
is an (unoriented) ideal of
S
, and
\delta
is an invertible element of
S\otimes\mathbb{Q}
such that
I^{3}\subset\delta\cdot S
and
N(I)^{3}=N(\delta)
. Two triples
(S,I,\delta)
and
(S^{\prime},I^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})
are said to be equivalent if there exists an isomorphism
\phi:S\rightarrow S^{\prime}
and an element
\kappa\in S^{\prime}\otimes\mathbb{Q}
such that
I^{\prime}=\kappa\phi(I)
and
\delta^{\prime}=\kappa^{3}\phi(\delta)
. Under this bijection, the discriminant of a binary cubic form
f
equals the discriminant of the corresponding oriented quadratic ring, and the projective binary cubic forms correspond to triples
(S,I,\delta)
such that
I
is a projective
S
-module. Since, the classes of triples
(S,I,\delta)
have a natural structure of an abelian group, the set of projective
\text{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
-orbits of
\text{Sym}^{3}\mathbb{Z}^{2}
of a fixed discriminant
D
is also endowed with the structure of an abelian group, which is denoted by
\text{Cl}(\text{Sym}^{3}\mathbb{Z}^{2};D)
. The identity element of the group
[f_{\text{id},D}](=[\text{id}])
is given by the equivalence class of the form

f_{\text{id},D}=\begin{cases}3x^{2}y+\frac{D}{4}y^{3}&\text{if }D\equiv 0\text{ (mod }4)\\
3x^{2}y+3xy^{2}+\frac{D+3}{4}y^{3}&\text{if }D\equiv 1\text{ (mod }4)\end{cases}

corresponding to the triply symmetric cubes
A_{\text{id},D}
given in (
9
).

We now explain how the bijection is constructed. Suppose we start with a triple
(S,I,\delta)
as above. Let
\langle 1,\tau\rangle
be a positively oriented
\mathbb{Z}
-basis of
S
, where
\tau
satisfies (
19
). Let
\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle
be a positively oriented
\mathbb{Z}
-basis for
I
. Since
I^{3}\subset\delta\cdot S
, there exist
a_{i},a_{i}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}
, for
i=0,1,2,3
such that

(38)

\begin{split}\alpha^{3}&=\delta(a_{0}^{\prime}+a_{0}\tau)\\
\alpha^{2}\beta&=\delta(a_{1}^{\prime}+a_{1}\tau)\\
\alpha\beta^{2}&=\delta(a_{2}^{\prime}+a_{2}\tau)\\
\beta^{3}&=\delta(a_{3}^{\prime}+a_{3}\tau).\end{split}

The binary cubic form corresponding to the triple
(S,I,\delta)
is given by

f(x,y)=a_{0}x^{3}+3a_{1}x^{2}y+3a_{2}xy^{2}+a_{3}y^{3}.

The binary cubic form
f^{\prime}(x,y)=a_{0}^{\prime}x^{3}+3a_{1}^{\prime}x^{2}y+3a_{2}^{\prime}xy^{2}+a_{3}^{\prime}y^{3}
is called the companion form of
f(x,y)
, and it is easily verified that the cube
A_{f^{\prime}}
is the companion cube of
A_{f}
defined in (
25
).
In Section
4.3
, we will see that the companion binary cubic form
f^{\prime}
is a covariant of
f
and there is a syzygy connecting them.

In the other direction, to get a triple
(S,I_{f},\delta_{f})
corresponding to a given binary cubic form
f(x,y)\in\text{Sym}^{3}\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, we solve the system of equations in (
38
). The numbers
a^{\prime}_{0},a^{\prime}_{1},a^{\prime}_{2},a^{\prime}_{3}
are determined in terms of
a_{0},a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}
as given on page 238 in
[
3
]
. Then, the values of
\alpha
and
\beta
are determined uniquely up to multiplication by a scalar
\kappa\in K
. The scalar
\delta_{f}
is uniquely determined by
\alpha
and
\beta
, and it gets multiplied by
\kappa^{3}
if
\alpha
and
\beta
are multiplied by
\kappa
. A possible choice of values for
\alpha,\beta
and
\delta_{f}
are

\begin{split}\alpha&=a^{\prime}_{1}+a_{1}\tau,\\
\beta&=a^{\prime}_{2}+a_{2}\tau,\\
\delta_{f}&=\alpha\beta.\end{split}

It follows from (
38
) that for
x,y\in\mathbb{Z}
, we can write

(x\alpha+y\beta)^{3}=\delta_{f}(f(x,y)\tau+f^{\prime}(x,y)).

We can express the group law on
\text{Cl}(\text{Sym}^{3}\mathbb{Z}^{2};D)
in terms of the group law on cubes
\text{Cl}(\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{Z}^{2};D)
as follows.

Proposition 4.1
.

Let
f,g,h
be three projective binary cubic forms of discriminant
D
in
\mathrm{Sym}^{3}\mathbb{Z}^{2}
with corresponding triply-symmetric cubes
A_{f},A_{g}
and
A_{h},
respectively. Then

[f]+[g]+[h]=[{\mathtt{id}}]

in
\mathrm{Cl}(\mathrm{Sym}^{3}\mathbb{Z}^{2};D)
if and only if

[A_{f}]+[A_{g}]+[A_{h}]=[{\mathtt{id}}]

in
\mathrm{Cl}(\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{Z}^{2};D)
and
\delta_{f}\delta_{g}\delta_{h}
is in
K^{\times 3}
, where
K^{\times 3}
denotes the group of cubes of units of
K
.

Proof.

Let
f,g
and
h
correspond to the triples
(S,I_{1},\delta_{f}),(S,I_{2},\delta_{g})
and
(S,I_{3},\delta_{h})
respectively. The cubes
A_{f},A_{g}
and
A_{h}
correspond to the balanced triples of ideals
(I_{1},I_{1},\delta_{f}^{-1}I_{1}),(I_{2},I_{2},\delta_{g}^{-1}I_{2})
and
(I_{3},I_{3},\delta_{h}^{-1}I_{3})
respectively. Then,
[f]+[g]+[h]=[\text{id}]
if and only if
I_{1}I_{2}I_{3}=(\delta)
and
\delta_{f}\delta_{g}\delta_{h}=\delta^{3}
for some
\delta\in K^{\times}
, which is equivalent to
[A_{f}]+[A_{g}]+[A_{h}]=[{\mathtt{id}}]
.
∎

We note that the binary quadratic forms
Q_{i}^{A_{f}}
associated to the triply symmetric cube
A_{f}
satisfy
Q_{1}^{A_{f}}=Q_{2}^{A_{f}}=Q_{3}^{A_{f}}
and we denote the common binary quadratic form by
Q_{f}
.

4.2.
Explicit composition for binary cubic forms

We now define the form product of two projective binary cubic forms of common discriminant
D
. For two such forms
f
and
g
, define

\displaystyle(f\star g)((x,y);(u,v))=f(x,y)g^{\prime}(u,v)+f^{\prime}(x,y)g(u,v)+\epsilon f(x,y)g(u,v)

where as usual,
\epsilon=0
or
1
is congruent to
D
mod 4. Our main theorem for binary cubic forms is the following.

Theorem 4.2
.

Let
f,g,h\in\text{Sym}^{3}\mathbb{Z}^{2}
be three projective binary cubic forms of common discriminant
D
and
\epsilon\in\{0,1\}
congruent to
D
mod 4. Let
x,y,u,v\in\mathbb{Z}.
Then

[f]+[g]+[h]=[{\mathtt{id}}]

if and only if there exists a cube
R
of discriminant
D
satisfying

\displaystyle(g\star h)

\displaystyle((x,y);(u,v))

\displaystyle=f(R^{\sigma}((x,y),(u,v))).

and

(39)

Q_{1}^{R}=Q^{f},Q_{2}^{R}=Q^{g},

and

(40)

\displaystyle Q^{g}(1,0)\cdot Q^{h}(1,0)

\displaystyle=Q^{f}(r_{211},r_{111}).

Proof.

Suppose that
[f]+[g]+[h]=[{\mathtt{id}}].
Then
[A_{f}]+[A_{g}]+[A_{h}]=[{\mathtt{id}}]
in
\text{Cl}(\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{Z}^{2};D).
By Theorem
1.3
, there exist cubes
R,S,T
of discriminant
D
satisfying

(41)

\displaystyle(A_{g}\star A_{h})

\displaystyle(\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z};\underline{u},\underline{v},\underline{w})

\displaystyle=A_{f}(R^{\sigma}(\underline{x},\underline{u}),S^{\sigma}(\underline{y},\underline{v}),T^{\sigma}(\underline{z},\underline{w}))

and satisfying (
39
) and (
40
). In fact, it follows from the proof on Theorem
1.3
that we can take
R=S=T.
Indeed, let the three cubes
A_{f},A_{g},A_{h}
correspond to the oriented triples of ideals
(I_{1},I_{1},\delta_{f}^{-1}I_{1}),(I_{2},I_{2},\delta_{g}^{-1}I_{2})
and
(I_{3},I_{3},\delta_{h}^{-1}I_{3})
, respectively. Then,
R
and
S
will both correspond to
(I_{1},I_{2},I_{3})
and
T
to
(\delta_{f}^{-1}I_{1},\delta_{g}^{-1}I_{2},\delta_{h}^{-1}I_{3}).
Assuming without loss of generality that
\delta_{f}\delta_{g}\delta_{h}=1
we see that
R=S=T.

Thus, taking
\underline{x}=\underline{y}=\underline{z}
and
\underline{u}=\underline{v}=\underline{w}
in (
41
), we conclude

(g\star h)((x,y);(u,v))=f(R^{\sigma}((x,y),(u,v))).

Equation (
40
) follows from applying equation (
8
) to the cube
R
.

Conversely, let us assume that

(42)

(g\star h)((x,y);(u,v))=f(R^{\sigma}((x,y),(u,v)))

and (
39
) and (
40
) are satisfied. We will show that the two multilinear forms

(43)

(A_{g}\star A_{h})(\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z};\underline{u},\underline{v},\underline{w})

and

(44)

\displaystyle A_{f}(R^{\sigma}(\underline{x},\underline{u}),R^{\sigma}(\underline{y},\underline{v}),R^{\sigma}(\underline{z},\underline{w}))

in
\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z},\underline{u},\underline{v},\underline{w}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
are equal. Both are symmetric in
\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z}
and in
\underline{u},\underline{v},\underline{w}
— this is obvious in the first case, and a routine computation in the second case. The polarization formula applied to (
42
) implies that (
43
) and (
44
) are equal. See
[
20
]
Lemma 4.6, for example.
We conclude that

[A_{f}]+[A_{g}]+[A_{h}]=[{\mathtt{id}}]
by again appealing to Theorem
1.3
.

As before let the three cubes
A_{f},A_{g},A_{h}
correspond to the oriented triples of ideals
(I_{1},I_{1},\delta_{f}^{-1}I_{1}),(I_{2},I_{2},\delta_{g}^{-1}I_{2})
and
(I_{3},I_{3},\delta_{h}^{-1}I_{3})
, respectively. Choose
\mathbb{Z}
-bases
\langle\alpha_{j},\beta_{j}\rangle
of
I_{j}
, for
j=1,2,3.
The cube
\tilde{A_{f}}
corresponds to the triple
(I_{1}^{-1},I_{1}^{-1},\delta_{f}I_{1}^{-1})
, and a compatible choice of basis is given by

\langle\alpha_{1}^{\prime},-\beta_{1}^{\prime}\rangle
where
\alpha_{1}^{\prime}=N(I_{1})^{-1}\overline{\alpha_{1}}
and
\beta_{1}^{\prime}=N(I_{1})^{-1}\overline{\beta_{1}}.

From (
42
), we get

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\delta_{g}}(x\alpha_{2}+y\beta_{2})^{3}\frac{1}{\delta_{h}}(u\alpha_{3}+v\beta_{3})^{3}={\delta_{f}}(a\alpha_{1}^{\prime}+b\beta_{1}^{\prime})^{3},

where
(a,b)=R^{\iota}((x,y),(u,v)).
Substituting
x=u=1,y=v=0
in the above equation, we get

\displaystyle\frac{\alpha_{2}^{3}\alpha_{3}^{3}}{\delta_{g}\delta_{h}}=\delta_{f}(r_{211}\alpha_{3}+r_{111}\beta_{3})^{3}.

Hence,
\delta_{f}\delta_{g}\delta_{h}
is a cube of an element of
K
. This fact combined with
[A_{f}]+[A_{g}]+[A_{h}]=[{\mathtt{id}}]
allows us to conclude that
[f]+[g]+[h]=[\text{id}]
, as desired.

∎

Remark 4.3
.

In
[
23
]
, the definition of composition of two binary cubic forms is essentially identical to what we defined as form product of the cubic forms. We note that the
\mathbb{Z}
-basis of
S(D)
used in
[
23
]
is slightly different from our choice.

We also point out that Theorem 1.1 in
[
23
]
is stated incorrectly. If
f,g
and
h
are projective binary cubic forms of common discriminant
D
satisfying the composition identity (
42
), then it is not true that
[f]+[g]+[h]=[\mathrm{id}]
in
\mathrm{Cl}(\text{Sym}^{3}\mathbb{Z}^{2};D)
as is claimed in
[
23
]
. This can be seen by replacing
f
by
\tilde{f}
, which is the form in the inverse class of
f
, in the identity (
42
) and changing the bilinear form accordingly. (One needs to assume the conditions on the bilinear form given by the cube
R
as stated in our Theorem
4.2
in order to derive the conclusion
[f]+[g]+[h]=[\mathrm{id}]
.)

4.3.
Companion cubic form and syzygy

The binary cubic form
T_{f}(x,y)
defined by

T_{f}(x,y)=f^{\prime}(x,y)+\frac{\epsilon}{2}f(x,y),

is classically referred to as the “cubicovariant” of the binary cubic form
f
. The binary cubic forms
f^{\prime},T_{f}
and the quadratic form
Q_{f}
associated to the triply symmetric cube
A_{f}
are all covariants of
f
. Furthermore,
they are connected via the following syzygy, which is due to Eisenstein (
[
15
]
):

T_{f}(x,y)^{2}-\frac{D}{4}f(x,y)^{2}=Q_{f}(x,-y)^{3}.

Here
D
is the discriminant of
f(x,y)
(and
Q_{f}(x,y)
).

4.4.
Example

We demonstrate an example of the composition identity involving binary cubic forms.

Let
f(x,y)=3x^{2}y+2y^{3},g(x,y)=x^{3}+3x^{2}y+6xy^{2}+2y^{3}
and
h(x,y)=x^{3}+3xy^{2}+2y^{3}.
Then,
f,g
and
h
have discriminant
8
and satisfy
[f]+[g]+[h]=[\text{id}]
in
\text{Cl}(\text{Sym}^{3}\mathbb{Z}^{2};8)
. The companion cubic forms are
f^{\prime}(x,y)=x^{3}+6xy^{2},g^{\prime}(x,y)=-x^{3}-6x^{2}y-6xy^{2}-4y^{3}
and
h^{\prime}(x,y)=x^{3}-3x^{2}y-3xy^{2}-3y^{3}
. The binary quadratic forms
Q^{f},Q^{g},Q^{h}
are given by
Q^{f}=[1,0,-2],Q^{g}=[-1,0,2],Q^{h}=[-1,2,1]
.

The cube
R
given by

R=\hbox to93.89pt{\vbox to84.81pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 8.61076pt\lower-6.55522pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

\par{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{81.9433pt}{17.92503pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{

is dual to the triple
(f,g,h)
, and we have the composition identity

\displaystyle(g\star h)

\displaystyle((x,y);(u,v))=f(R^{\sigma}((x,y),(u,v))).

5.
Composition of pairs of binary quadratic forms

\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\text{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{Z}^{2}
of pairs of binary quadratic forms with even middle coefficients. An element
F=(F_{1},F_{2})=(ax^{2}+2bxy+cy^{2},dx^{2}+2exy+fy^{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\text{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{Z}^{2}
can be represented as a doubly symmetric cube given by

The group
\text{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})\times\text{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
acts on the space
\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\text{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{Z}^{2}
in the following way. If
(g,h)\in\text{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})\times\text{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
and
(F_{1},F_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\text{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, then

(45)

(g,h)\cdot(F_{1},F_{2})=(p(h^{t}\cdot F_{1})+q(h^{t}\cdot F_{2}),r(h^{t}\cdot F_{1})+s(h^{t}\cdot F_{2})),

where
g=\begin{pmatrix}p&q\\
r&s\end{pmatrix}
,
h^{t}
denotes transpose of
h
, and the action of
h^{t}
on
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
is as given in equation (
4
). Under the inclusion map
\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\text{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{Z}^{2}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, the action given in equation (
45
) corresponds to
(g,h,h)\in\Gamma
acting on the doubly symmetric cube
A_{F}
corresponding to
F=(F_{1},F_{2})
.
We note that the binary quadratic forms
Q_{i}^{A_{F}}
associated to the doubly symmetric cube
A_{F}
satisfy
Q_{2}^{A_{F}}=Q_{3}^{A_{F}}
.
The discriminant of the cube is the unique polynomial invariant for this action.
The pair
F=(F_{1},F_{2})
is called projective (resp. nondegenerate) if the cube
A_{F}
is projective (resp. nondegenerate).

5.1.
Correspondence between pairs of binary quadratic forms and pairs of ideals

With the same notation as Section
2
, we briefly describe the correspondence between pairs of binary quadratic forms and certain balanced triples of oriented ideals.

There is a canonical bijection between nondegenerate
\text{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})\times\text{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
-orbits of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\text{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{Z}^{2}
and isomorphism classes of pairs
(S,(I_{1},I_{2},I_{3}))
, where
S
is a nondegenerate oriented quadratic ring and
(I_{1},I_{2},I_{3})
is an equivalence class of balanced triples of oriented ideals of
S
such that
I_{2}=I_{3}
. Under this bijection, the discriminant of an element of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\text{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{Z}^{2}
equals the discriminant of the corresponding oriented quadratic ring.
The proof of this bijection is similar to the case of
2\times 2\times 2
cubes.

The projective
\text{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})\times\text{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
-orbits of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\text{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{Z}^{2}
correspond to pairs
(S,(I_{1},I_{2},I_{2}))
, where
I_{1}
and
I_{2}
are projective
S
-modules. Hence, the set of projective
\text{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})\times\text{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
-orbits of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\text{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{Z}^{2}
of a fixed discriminant
D
is equpped with the structure of an abelian group, and this group is denoted by
\text{Cl}(\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\text{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{Z}^{2};D)
. The identity element of the group is the class of the pair
F_{\text{id},D}
given by

F_{\text{id},D}=\begin{cases}\left(2xy,x^{2}+\frac{D}{4}y^{2}\right)&\text{if }D\equiv 0\text{ (mod }4)\\
\left(2xy+y^{2},x^{2}+2xy+\frac{D+3}{4}y^{2}\right)&\text{if }D\equiv 1\text{ (mod }4).\end{cases}

We see that the ideal class of
I_{1}
in a balanced triple
(I_{1},I_{2},I_{2})
is determined by the ideal class of
I_{2}
. Hence, we get the following isomorphism:

\text{Cl}(\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\text{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{Z}^{2};D)\cong\text{Cl}^{+}(S(D)).

Let
F=(F_{1},F_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\text{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{Z}^{2}
and let
\underline{x}=(x_{1},x_{2})^{t},\underline{y}=(y_{1},y_{2})^{t}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. Then we denote
F(\underline{x},\underline{y})
to be

F(\underline{x},\underline{y})=x_{1}F_{1}(y_{1},y_{2})+x_{2}F_{2}(y_{1},y_{2}).

5.2.
Composition identities for pairs of binary quadratic forms

Let
F=(F_{1},F_{2})
and
G=(G_{1},G_{2})
be two pairs of binary quadratic forms in
\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\text{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{Z}^{2}
of common discriminant
D
. We define their form product
F\star G
to be

\displaystyle(F\star G)((\underline{x},\underline{y});(\underline{u},\underline{v}))=F(\underline{x},\underline{y})G^{\prime}(\underline{u},\underline{v})+F^{\prime}(\underline{x},\underline{y})G(\underline{u},\underline{v})+\epsilon F(\underline{x},\underline{y})G(\underline{u},\underline{v}),

for
\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{u},\underline{v}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}

We have the following theorem on composition of pairs of binary quadratic forms.

Theorem 5.1
.

Let
F=(F_{1},F_{2}),G=(G_{1},G_{2}),H=(H_{1},H_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{Z}^{2}
be three projective pairs of binary quadratic forms of common discriminant
D
and
\epsilon\in\{0,1\}
congruent to
D
mod
4
. Let
\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{u},\underline{v}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. Then

[F]+[G]+[H]=[\mathrm{id}]

if and only if there exist two cubes
R
and
S
of discriminant
D
satisfying

\displaystyle(G\star H)((\underline{x},\underline{y});(\underline{u},\underline{v}))=F(R^{\sigma}(\underline{x},\underline{u}),S(\underline{y},\underline{v}))

and

\displaystyle Q_{1}^{R}=Q_{1}^{F},\ Q_{2}^{R}=Q_{1}^{G},

and

\begin{split}Q_{1}^{G}(1,0)Q_{1}^{H}(1,0)=Q_{1}^{F}(r_{211},r_{111}),\\
Q_{2}^{G}(1,0)Q_{2}^{H}(1,0)=Q_{2}^{F}(s_{211},s_{111}).\end{split}

Proof.

We omit the proof which is similar to that of Theorem
1.3
.

∎

5.3.
Example

We consider the three elements
F=(F_{1},F_{2}),G=(G_{1},G_{2}),H=(H_{1},H_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\text{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{Z}^{2}
given by

\begin{split}(F_{1}(x,y),F_{2}(x,y)&=(80xy-63y^{2},x^{2}-30xy+23y^{2}),\\
(G_{1}(x,y),G_{2}(x,y))&=(-9x^{2}-2xy+y^{2},10x^{2}+4xy-y^{2}),\\
(H_{1}(x,y),H_{2}(x,y))&=(2x^{2}-y^{2},-x^{2}-4xy+y^{2}).\end{split}

Then,
F,G
and
H
have discriminant
-31
and satisfy

[F]+[G]+[H]=[\text{id}]

in
\text{Cl}(\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\text{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{Z}^{2};-31)
. The corresponding companion pairs are given by

\begin{split}(F_{1}^{\prime}(x,y),F_{2}^{\prime}(x,y)&=(1600x^{2}-2560xy+1016y^{2},-569x^{2}+910xy-361y^{2}),\\
(G_{1}^{\prime}(x,y),G_{2}^{\prime}(x,y))&=(x^{2}+18xy+y^{2},6x^{2}-20xy-2y^{2}),\\
(H_{1}^{\prime}(x,y),H_{2}^{\prime}(x,y))&=(-8xy+y^{2},-8x^{2}+8xy+3y^{2}).\end{split}

We have the composition identity

(G\star H)((\underline{x},\underline{y});(\underline{u},\underline{v}))=F(R^{\sigma}(\underline{x},\underline{u}),S^{\sigma}(\underline{y},\underline{v})),

where the cubes
R
and
S
are given by

6.
Composition of pairs of quaternary alternating 2-forms

The last two composition laws are defined on spaces obtained from the space of
2\times 2\times 2
cubes by skew-symmetrization operations. The first is the space
\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\wedge^{2}\mathbb{Z}^{4}
of pairs of quaternary alternating
2
-forms obtained by applying a double skew-symmetry operation on cubes. In this case, there is a natural map

\phi:\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{Z}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\wedge^{2}\mathbb{Z}^{4}

defined in Section 2.6 of
[
3
]
. To describe this map we identify
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
with its
\mathbb{Z}
-dual and let
\underline{x},\underline{y_{1}},\underline{y_{2}},\underline{z_{1}}.\underline{z_{2}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}.
For a cube
A\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{Z}^{2}
define

(46)

\phi(A)(\underline{x},(\underline{y_{1}},\underline{y_{2}}),(\underline{z_{1}},\underline{z_{2}}))=A(\underline{x},\underline{y_{1}},\underline{z_{2}})-A(\underline{x},\underline{z_{1}},\underline{y_{2}}).

After making a choice of basis, this map is explicitly given by

(47)

\hbox to91.25pt{\vbox to86.28pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.97595pt\lower-6.80522pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
\par{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{49.06879pt}{47.7424pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
