\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\rightarrow\left(\begin{bmatrix}&&a&b\\
&&c&d\\
-a&-c&&\\
-b&-d&&\end{bmatrix},\begin{bmatrix}&&e&f\\
&&g&h\\
-e&-g&&\\
-f&-h&&\end{bmatrix}\right).

The group
\text{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})\times\text{SL}_{4}(\mathbb{Z})
has a natural action on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\wedge^{2}\mathbb{Z}^{4}
and this action has a unique polynomial invariant, again called the discriminant of a pair
(F_{1},F_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\wedge^{2}\mathbb{Z}^{4}
.
It is given by
\text{disc}(\text{Pfaffian}(F_{1}x-F_{2}y))
, where the Pfaffian of a
4\times 4
skew-symmetric matrix
M
is given by

\text{Pfaffian}(M)=\sqrt{\text{Det}(M)}.

The choice of sign in the square-root is such that
\text{Pfaffian}\left(\begin{pmatrix}0&I\\
-I&0\end{pmatrix}\right)=1,
where
I
is the
2\times 2
identity matrix.
A pair
(F_{1},F_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\wedge^{2}\mathbb{Z}^{4}
is said to be projective if it is
\text{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})\times\text{SL}_{4}(\mathbb{Z})
-equivalent to a pair which is the image of a projective cube under the mapping
\phi:\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{Z}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\wedge^{2}\mathbb{Z}^{4}
of (
47
). The map
\phi
is clearly not surjective, but Bhargava shows that the map is surjective onto
\text{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})\times\text{SL}_{4}(\mathbb{Z})
equivalence classes (Section 3.6 of
[
3
]
).

6.1.
Correspondence between pairs of quaternary alternating 2-forms and pairs of ideals

To describe the correspondence between the forms and ideals, we need to recall some notation from (
[
3
]
).
Let
S
be an oriented quadratic ring and let
K=S\otimes\mathbb{Q}
.

Definition 6.1
.

A
rank
n
ideal
of
S
is an
S
-submodule of
K^{n}
having rank
2n
as a
\mathbb{Z}
-module. An oriented rank
n
ideal of
S(D)
is a pair
(M,\epsilon)
, where
M
is any rank
n
ideal of
S(D)
and
\epsilon=\pm 1
gives the orientation of
M
.

The norm of an oriented rank
n
ideal
(M,\epsilon)
is defined to be
\epsilon\cdot|L/M|\cdot|L/S^{n}|^{-1}
, where
L
denotes any lattice in
K^{n}
containing both
S^{n}
and
M
. We will call the usual rank 1 (fractional) ideals simply as ideals. The product of two oriented rank
n
ideals
(M_{1},\epsilon_{1})
and
(M_{2},\epsilon_{2})
is defined to be
(M_{1}M_{2},\epsilon_{1}\epsilon_{2})
, where
M_{1}M_{2}
is the component-wise multiplication in
K^{n}
. Two rank
n
ideals
(M_{1},\epsilon_{1})
and
(M_{2},\epsilon_{2})
are said to be equivalent if there exists
\lambda\in\text{GL}_{n}(K)
such that
(M_{1},\epsilon_{1})=(\lambda M_{2},\text{sgn}(N(\text{det}(\lambda)))\epsilon_{2})
.

Now, let
M
be an oriented rank 2 ideal of
S
. There is a canonical map
\mathrm{det}:(K^{2})^{2}\rightarrow K
given by

\mathrm{det}(\underline{X},\underline{Y})=\begin{vmatrix}X_{1}&X_{2}\\
Y_{1}&Y_{2}\end{vmatrix},

where
\underline{X}=(X_{1},X_{2})
and
\underline{Y}=(Y_{1},Y_{2})
are in
K^{2}
. Let
\mathrm{Det}(M)
denote the ideal in
S
generated by elements of the form
\mathrm{det}(\underline{X},\underline{Y})
for
\underline{X},\underline{Y}\in M
.
A pair
(I,M)
, where
I
and
M
are oriented ideals of
S
having ranks 1 and 2, is called
balanced
if
I\cdot\mathrm{Det}(M)\subset S
and
N(I)N(M)=1
. Two such balanced pairs
(I,M)
and
(I^{\prime},M^{\prime})
are said to be equivalent if there exist
\lambda_{1}\in\mathrm{GL}_{1}(K)
and
\lambda_{2}\in\mathrm{GL}_{2}(K)
such that
I^{\prime}=\lambda_{1}I
and
M^{\prime}=\lambda_{2}M
.

As proved in (
[
3
]
), there is a canonical bijection between the set of nondegenerate
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})\times\mathrm{SL}_{4}(\mathbb{Z})
-orbits on the space
\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\wedge^{2}\mathbb{Z}^{4}
of pairs of quaternary alternating
2
-forms and the set of isomorphism classes of pairs of
(S,(I,M))
, where
S
is a nondegenerate oriented quadratic ring and
(I,M)
is an equivalence class of balanced pairs of ideals of
S
having ranks 1 and 2 respectively. Under this bijection, the discriminant of a pair of quaternary alternating 2-forms is equal to the discriminant of the corresponding quadratic ring.

Given a pair
(S,(I,M))
, we now describe how to construct a corresponding pair of quaternary alternating 2-forms. Let
\langle 1,\tau\rangle
be a positively oriented
\mathbb{Z}
-basis of
S
, and let
\langle\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\rangle
and
\langle\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\beta_{3},\beta_{4}\rangle
be appropriately oriented
\mathbb{Z}
-bases of
I
and
M
respectively. Since
(I,M)
is balanced, we can write

(48)

\alpha_{i}\mathrm{det}(\beta_{j},\beta_{k})=c^{(i)}_{jk}+a^{(i)}_{jk}\tau,

for some
c^{(i)}_{jk},a^{(i)}_{jk}\in\mathbb{Z}
satisfying

c^{(i)}_{jk}=-c^{(i)}_{kj},\ a^{(i)}_{jk}=-a^{(i)}_{kj},

for
i=1,2
and
j,k=1,2,3,4
. The pair of forms in
\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\wedge^{2}\mathbb{Z}^{4}
corresponding to
(I,M)
is given by

(F_{1},F_{2})=\left(\sum_{j,k=1}^{4}a_{jk}^{(1)}e_{j}\wedge e_{k},\sum_{j,k=1}^{4}a_{jk}^{(2)}e_{j}\wedge e_{k}\right),

where
\langle e_{1},e_{2},e_{3},e_{4}\rangle
is the standard basis of
\mathbb{Z}^{4}
. The pair
(F_{1}^{\prime},F_{2}^{\prime})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\wedge^{2}\mathbb{Z}^{4}
given by

(F_{1}^{\prime},F_{2}^{\prime})=\left(\sum_{j,k=1}^{4}c_{jk}^{(1)}e_{j}\wedge e_{k},\sum_{j,k=1}^{4}c_{jk}^{(2)}e_{j}\wedge e_{k}\right)

is called the
companion pair
of
(F_{1},F_{2})
.

Let
\underline{x}=(x_{1},x_{2})^{t}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
and
\underline{y}=(y_{1},y_{2},y_{3},y_{4})^{t},\underline{z}=(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3},z_{4})^{t}\in\mathbb{Z}^{4}
. Using equation (
48
), we can write

(49)

(x_{1}\alpha_{1}+x_{2}\alpha_{2})\cdot\text{det}(\underline{Y},\underline{Z})=[x_{1}F_{1}(\underline{y},\underline{z})+x_{2}F_{2}(\underline{y},\underline{z})]\tau+[x_{1}F_{1}^{\prime}(\underline{y},\underline{z})+x_{2}F_{2}^{\prime}(\underline{y},\underline{z})],

where
\underline{Y}=\sum_{j=1}^{4}y_{j}\beta_{j},\underline{Z}=\sum_{j=1}^{4}z_{j}\beta_{j}
. As a shorthand, we will write

F(\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z})=x_{1}F_{1}(\underline{y},\underline{z})+x_{2}F_{2}(\underline{y},\underline{z}).

A pair of forms
(F_{1},F_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\wedge^{2}\mathbb{Z}^{4}
is
projective
if and only if the ideals in the corresponding pair
(I,M)
are projective
S
-modules.
Using results of Bass (
[
1
]
) and Serre (
[
25
]
), Bhargava shows that any projective balanced pair of ideals
(I,M)
is equivalent to a pair of the form
(I,S\oplus I^{-1})
. Thus, the set of equivalence classes of projective balanced pair of ideals
(I,M)
of a quadratic ring
S
is endowed with an abelian group structure given by

[(I_{1},S\oplus I_{1}^{-1})]*[(I_{2},S\oplus I_{2}^{-1})]=[(I_{1}I_{2},S\oplus(I_{1}I_{2})^{-1})].

Hence, the projective orbits of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\wedge^{2}\mathbb{Z}^{4}
of a fixed discriminant
D
under
\text{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})\times\text{SL}_{4}(\mathbb{Z})
action have a natural group structure via the correspondence with balanced pairs
(I,M)
. This group is denoted as
\text{Cl}(\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\wedge^{2}\mathbb{Z}^{4};D)
, and we have the following isomorphism:

\text{Cl}(\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\wedge^{2}\mathbb{Z}^{4};D)\cong\text{Cl}^{+}(S(D)).

This follows from the fact that any projective balanced pair
(I,M)
is equivalent to one of the form
(I,S\oplus I^{-1}).

Similar to Proposition
3.3
, we have the following proposition which provides the relation between a pair of forms
(F_{1},F_{2})
and its companion pair
(F_{1}^{\prime},F_{2}^{\prime}).

First we note that the
S
-module structure of
I
is given by equation (
24
) for
p_{1},q_{1},r_{1}
defined by

\text{Pfaffian}(F_{1}x-F_{2}y)=p_{1}x^{2}+q_{1}xy+r_{1}y^{2}.

In the proposition below we set
\underline{x}^{\prime}=(x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2}^{\prime})^{t}
as in equation (
27
).

Proposition 6.1
.

Let
F=(F_{1},F_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\wedge^{2}\mathbb{Z}^{4}
be a pair of quaternary alternating
2
-forms and let
(F_{1}^{\prime},F_{2}^{\prime})
be its companion pair of forms. We have

F^{\prime}(\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z})=F(\underline{x}^{\prime},\underline{y},\underline{z})-\epsilon F(\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z}).

Proof.

Multiplying both sides of equation (
49
) by
\tau
, we get

\tau(x_{1}\alpha_{1}+x_{2}\alpha_{2})\cdot\text{det}(\underline{Y},\underline{Z})=F(\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z})\tau^{2}+F^{\prime}(\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z})\tau.

Thus

(x_{1}^{\prime}\alpha_{1}+x_{2}^{\prime}\alpha_{2})\cdot\text{det}(\underline{Y},\underline{Z})=F(\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z})\tau^{2}+F^{\prime}(\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z})\tau.

Replacing
\underline{x}
by
\underline{x}^{\prime}
in (
49
), we also have

(x_{1}^{\prime}\alpha_{1}+x_{2}^{\prime}\alpha_{2})\cdot\text{det}(\underline{Y},\underline{Z})=F(\underline{x}^{\prime},\underline{y},\underline{z})\tau+F^{\prime}(\underline{x}^{\prime},\underline{y},\underline{z}).

Combining the last two equations gives us

F(\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z})\tau^{2}+F^{\prime}(\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z})\tau=F(\underline{x}^{\prime},\underline{y},\underline{z})\tau+F^{\prime}(\underline{x}^{\prime},\underline{y},\underline{z}).

Finally, using equation (
19
) and equating the coefficients of
\tau
on both sides of the above equation completes the proof of the Proposition.
∎

6.2.
Composition identities for pairs of alternating quaternary forms

Let
F=(F_{1},F_{2})
and
G=(G_{1},G_{2})
be two pairs of quaternary alternating
2
-forms in
\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\wedge^{2}\mathbb{Z}^{4}
of common discriminant
D
and
\epsilon\in\{0,1\}
congruent to
D
mod 4. We define their form product
F\star G
to be

\displaystyle(F\star G)((\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z});(\underline{u},\underline{v},\underline{w}))=F(\underline{x},

\displaystyle\underline{y},\underline{z})G^{\prime}(\underline{u},\underline{v},\underline{w})

\displaystyle+F^{\prime}(\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z})G(\underline{u},\underline{v},\underline{w})+\epsilon F(\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z})G(\underline{u},\underline{v},\underline{w}),

for
\underline{x},\underline{u}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
and
\underline{y},\underline{z},\underline{v},\underline{w}\in\mathbb{Z}^{4}
.

Before we state our main result on composition of pairs of quaternary alternating
2
-forms, we need the following proposition. We recall that the map
\phi
was defined by equation (
47
).

Proposition 6.2
.

Let
F=(F_{1},F_{2}),G=(G_{1},G_{2}),H=(H_{1},H_{2})
be three projective quaternary alternating
2
-forms of a common discriminant
D
satisfying

[F]+[G]+[H]=[\mathrm{id}]

in
\mathrm{Cl}(\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\wedge^{2}\mathbb{Z}^{4};D)
. Then there exist cubes
A,B,C
such that one of them is a doubly symmetric cube, and
\phi(A),\phi(B)
and
\phi(C)
are
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})\times\mathrm{SL}_{4}(\mathbb{Z})
-equivalent to
F,G
and
H
respectively.

Proof.

Let
S=S(D)
be the oriented quadratic ring of discriminant
D
. A result of Bass (
[
1
]
) implies that a balanced pair
(I,M)
, where
I
and
M
are ideals of
S
having ranks
1
and
2
respectively, is equivalent to a balanced pair of the form
(I,J\oplus K)
where
I,J,K
are fractional
S
-ideals. Hence, we may assume without loss of generality that there exist balanced pairs
(I_{1},I_{2}\oplus I_{3}),(J_{1},J_{2}\oplus J_{3})
and
(K_{1},K_{2}\oplus K_{3})
corresponding to
F,G,
and
H
respectively. Also,
I_{1}J_{1}K_{1}=S
because
[F]+[G]+[H]=[\text{id}]
.

We also recall that, by the cancellation theorem of Serre, a projective module of rank
2
is determined by its determinant. Hence, any two balanced pair
(I,M_{1})
and
(I,M_{2})
are equivalent because
\text{Det}(M_{1})=\text{Det}(M_{2})=I^{-1}
.

Let
Cl=\text{Cl}(S(D))
denote the (usual) class group of the quadratic ring
S(D)
. Let
Cl^{2}
denote the subgroup of squares in
Cl
. Then, we have the following two possibilities for the unoriented ideal classes of
I_{1},J_{1}
and
K_{1}
.

Case 1:
The ideal classes of one of
I_{1}
and
J_{1}
is in
Cl^{2}
.

Case 2:
The ideal classes of neither
I_{1}
and
J_{1}
is in
Cl^{2}
. In this case, the ideal class of
K_{1}
is in
Cl^{2}
, because
Cl^{2}
is an index two subgroup of
Cl
.

Hence, one of the ideals
I_{1},J_{1}
or
K_{1}
is equivalent to a square of an ideal. Let us say, without loss of generality, that
I_{1}
is equivalent to
L_{1}^{2}
for some ideal
L_{1}
. Then, the balanced pairs
(I_{1},I_{2}\oplus I_{3})
and
(L_{1}^{2},L_{1}^{-1}\oplus L_{1}^{-1})
are equivalent by Serre’s theorem. Hence,
F,G,H
are equivalent to
\phi(A),\phi(B),\phi(C)
, where
A,B
and
C
are the cubes corresponding to the triples
(L_{1}^{2},L_{1}^{-1},L_{1}^{-1}),(J_{1},J_{2},J_{3})
and
(K_{1},K_{2},K_{3})
respectively.

∎

Suppose
[F]+[G]+[H]=[{\mathtt{id}}]
in
\text{Cl}(\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\wedge^{2}\mathbb{Z}^{4};D)
. We say the representative forms
F,G,H
are well-chosen if

•

the three forms are in the image of
\phi
:
F=\phi(A),G=\phi(B),H=\phi(C)
for some cubes
A,B,C\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{Z}^{2}
,

•

[A]+[B]+[C]=[{\mathtt{id}}]
in
\text{Cl}(\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{Z}^{2};D)
and

•

A
is doubly-symmetric.

It is easy to see from Proposition
6.2
that given the relation
[F]+[G]+[H]=[{\mathtt{id}}]
, we can always replace
F,G,H
by well-chosen
\text{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})\times\text{SL}_{4}(\mathbb{Z})
equivalent representatives, after possibly permuting
F,G,H
.

Theorem 6.3
.

Let
F,G
and
H
be three projective pairs of quaternary alternating
2
-forms of common discriminant
D
and
\epsilon\in\{0,1\}
congruent to
D
mod 4.
Let
\underline{x},\underline{y_{i}},\underline{z_{i}},\underline{u},\underline{v_{i}},\underline{w_{i}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
and
\underline{y}=(\underline{y_{1}},\underline{y_{2}}),\underline{z}=(\underline{z_{1}},\underline{z_{2}}),\underline{v}=(\underline{v_{1}},\underline{v_{2}}),\underline{w}=(\underline{w_{1}},\underline{w_{2}})\in\mathbb{Z}^{4}.

Then

[F]+[G]+[H]=[{\mathtt{id}}],

with
F,G,H
well-chosen representatives (in the images of cubes
A,B,C
respectively)
if and only if there exist cubes
R=(r_{ijk}),S=(s_{ijk}),T=(t_{ijk})
of discriminant
D
satisfying

(50)

\displaystyle(G\star H)

\displaystyle(\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z};\underline{u},\underline{v},\underline{w})

\displaystyle=F

\displaystyle(R^{\sigma}(\underline{x},\underline{u}),(S^{\sigma}(\underline{y_{1}},\underline{v_{1}})+T^{\sigma}(\underline{y_{2}},\underline{v_{2}}),S^{\sigma}(\underline{y_{1}},\underline{w_{1}})+T^{\sigma}(\underline{y_{2}},\underline{w_{2}})),

\displaystyle(S^{\sigma}(\underline{z_{1}},\underline{v_{1}})+T^{\sigma}(\underline{z_{2}},\underline{v_{2}}),S^{\sigma}(\underline{z_{1}},\underline{w_{1}})+T^{\sigma}(\underline{z_{2}},\underline{w_{2}}))),

with

(51)

\displaystyle Q_{1}^{R}=Q_{1}^{A},\ Q_{2}^{R}=Q_{1}^{B},

and

(52)

\displaystyle Q_{1}^{B}(1,0)\cdot Q_{1}^{C}(1,0)

\displaystyle=Q_{1}^{A}(r_{211},r_{111}),

\displaystyle Q_{2}^{B}(1,0)\cdot Q_{2}^{C}(1,0)

\displaystyle=Q_{2}^{A}(s_{211},s_{111}),

\displaystyle Q_{3}^{B}(1,0)\cdot Q_{3}^{C}(1,0)

\displaystyle=Q_{3}^{A}(t_{211},t_{111}).

Proof.

We start by assuming that
[F]+[G]+[H]=[{\mathtt{id}}]
with
F=\phi(A),G=\phi(B),H=\phi(C)
with
A
doubly-symmetric and
[A]+[B]+[C]=[{\mathtt{id}}].
By Theorem
1.3
, this last condition implies that there exist cubes
R,S,T
such that

(53)

(B\star C)(\underline{x},\underline{y_{i}},\underline{z_{j}};\underline{u},\underline{v_{k}},\underline{w_{l}})=A(R^{\sigma}(\underline{x},\underline{u}),S^{\sigma}(\underline{y_{i}},\underline{v_{k}}),T^{\sigma}(\underline{z_{j}},\underline{w_{l}}))

and the normalizing conditions (
51
), (
52
) are satisfied.

Proposition
6.1
implies
\phi(B)^{\prime}=\phi(B^{\prime})
and
\phi(C)^{\prime}=\phi(C^{\prime})
. Hence

\displaystyle(G\star H)

\displaystyle(\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z};\underline{u},\underline{v},\underline{w})

\displaystyle=G(\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z})H^{\prime}(\underline{u},\underline{v},\underline{w})+G^{\prime}(\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z})H(\underline{u},\underline{v},\underline{w})+\epsilon G(\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z})H(\underline{u},\underline{v},\underline{w})

\displaystyle=\phi(B)(\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z})\phi(C^{\prime})(\underline{u},\underline{v},\underline{w})+\phi(B^{\prime})(\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z})\phi(C)(\underline{u},\underline{v},\underline{w})

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ +\epsilon\phi(B)(\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z})\phi(C)(\underline{u},\underline{v},\underline{w})

Using the definition (
46
) of
\phi
together with (
53
), a little algebra now yields (
50
).

Conversely, with
F,G,H\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\wedge^{2}\mathbb{Z}^{4}
and
A,B,C\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{Z}^{2}
be as in the statement of the theorem, assume that

(54)

\displaystyle(G\star H)

\displaystyle(\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z};\underline{u},\underline{v},\underline{w})

\displaystyle=F

\displaystyle(R^{\sigma}(\underline{x},\underline{u}),(S^{\sigma}(\underline{y_{1}},\underline{v_{1}})+T^{\sigma}(\underline{y_{2}},\underline{v_{2}}),S^{\sigma}(\underline{y_{1}},\underline{w_{1}})+T^{\sigma}(\underline{y_{2}},\underline{w_{2}})),

\displaystyle(S^{\sigma}(\underline{z_{1}},\underline{v_{1}})+T^{\sigma}(\underline{z_{2}},\underline{v_{2}}),S^{\sigma}(\underline{z_{1}},\underline{w_{1}})+T^{\sigma}(\underline{z_{2}},\underline{w_{2}}))),

is satisfied for some cubes
R,S,T
of discriminant
D
. We also assume (
51
), (
52
). We will prove that
[A]+[B]+[C]=[{\mathtt{id}}]
. The relation
[F]+[G]+[H]=[{\mathtt{id}}]
will then follow.

Observe that substituting
\underline{y_{2}}=\underline{z_{1}}=(0,0)
and
\underline{v_{2}}=\underline{w_{1}}=(0,0)
in (
50
) gives

\displaystyle(B\star C)(\underline{x},\underline{y_{1}},\underline{z_{2}};\underline{u},\underline{v_{1}},\underline{w_{2}})=A(R^{\sigma}(\underline{x},\underline{u}),S^{\sigma}(\underline{y_{1}},\underline{v_{1}}),T^{\sigma}(\underline{z_{2}},\underline{w_{2}})).

Under the assumptions stated above, by Theorem
1.3
, we immediately conclude that
[A]+[B]+[C]=[{\mathtt{id}}]
. This completes the proof of the theorem.

∎

6.3.

in
\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\wedge^{2}\mathbb{Z}^{4}
of discriminant
-47
which are equal to
\phi(A),\phi(B)
and
\phi(C)

We have

[A]+[B]+[C]=[{\mathtt{id}}]

in
\text{Cl}(\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{Z}^{2};-47)
, and the cube
A
is doubly-symmetric. Also, the associated quadratic forms are given by
Q_{1}^{A}=[4,-1,3],Q_{2}^{A}=[2,1,6],Q_{3}^{A}=[2,1,6],Q_{1}^{B}=[2,1,6],Q_{2}^{B}=[2,-1,6],Q_{3}^{B}=[1,-1,12],Q_{1}^{C}=[2,1,6],Q_{2}^{C}=[1,1,12]
and
Q_{3}^{C}=[7,25,24].

The companion pairs
F^{\prime},G^{\prime}
and
H^{\prime}
are given by
\phi(A^{\prime}),\phi(B^{\prime})
and
\phi(C^{\prime})
respectively, where
A^{\prime},B^{\prime},C^{\prime}
are the companion cubes of
A,B,C
respectively and are equal to

and the pair of forms
F,G,H
satisfy the composition identity

\displaystyle(G\star H)

\displaystyle(\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z};\underline{u},\underline{v},\underline{w})

\displaystyle=F

\displaystyle(R^{\sigma}(\underline{x},\underline{u}),(S^{\sigma}(\underline{y_{1}},\underline{v_{1}})+T^{\sigma}(\underline{y_{2}},\underline{v_{2}}),S^{\sigma}(\underline{y_{1}},\underline{w_{1}})+T^{\sigma}(\underline{y_{2}},\underline{w_{2}})),

\displaystyle(S^{\sigma}(\underline{z_{1}},\underline{v_{1}})+T^{\sigma}(\underline{z_{2}},\underline{v_{2}}),S^{\sigma}(\underline{z_{1}},\underline{w_{1}})+T^{\sigma}(\underline{z_{2}},\underline{w_{2}}))),

7.
Composition of senary alternating 3-forms

The final composition law we consider is the composition of senary alternating
3
-forms. The space
\wedge^{3}\mathbb{Z}^{6}
of senary alternating
3
-forms is obtained by imposing triple skew-symmetry on the space
\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. As in the previous section, we have a natural map

(55)

\wedge_{2,2,2}:\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{Z}^{2}\rightarrow\wedge^{3}(\mathbb{Z}^{2}\oplus\mathbb{Z}^{2}\oplus\mathbb{Z}^{2})=\wedge^{3}\mathbb{Z}^{6},

(See Section 2.7 of
[
3
]
for the definition of this map.)

The group
\text{SL}_{6}(\mathbb{Z})
acts on the space
\wedge^{3}\mathbb{Z}^{6}
and there is a unique polynomial invariant under this action called the discriminant. A form
E\in\wedge^{3}\mathbb{Z}^{6}
is called
nondegenerate
if its discriminant is nonzero and it is called
projective
if it is equivalent to a form which is in the image of the mapping
\wedge_{2,2,2}
.

7.1.
Correspondence between senary alternating 3-forms and rank 3 ideals

Let
S
be an oriented quadratic ring,
K=S\otimes\mathbb{Q}
, and let
M\subset K^{3}
be a rank 3 ideal of
S
. There is a canonical map
\mathrm{det}:(K^{3})^{3}\rightarrow K
, given by

\mathrm{det}(\underline{X},\underline{Y},\underline{Z})=\begin{vmatrix}X_{1}&X_{2}&X_{3}\\
Y_{1}&Y_{2}&Y_{3}\\
Z_{1}&Z_{2}&Z_{3}\end{vmatrix}.

Let
\mathrm{Det}(M)
be the ideal generated by elements of the form
\mathrm{det}(\underline{X},\underline{Y},\underline{Z})
, where
\underline{X},\underline{Y},\underline{Z}\in M
. If
M
is a decomposable ideal i.e.
M=I_{1}\oplus I_{2}\oplus I_{3}\subset K^{3}
, for some rank
1
ideals
I_{1},I_{2},I_{3}
of
S
, then
\mathrm{Det}(M)=I_{1}I_{2}I_{3}
. We say
M
is balanced if
\mathrm{Det}(M)\subset S
and
N(M)=1
. Two rank
3
ideals
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
are said to be equivalent if there exists
\lambda\in GL_{3}(K)
such that
\lambda\cdot M_{1}=M_{2}
.

Bhargava (
[
3
]
) proves there is a canonical bijection between the set of nondegenerate
\mathrm{SL}_{6}(\mathbb{Z})
-orbits on the space
\wedge^{3}\mathbb{Z}^{6}
of senary alternating
3
-forms, and the set of isomorphism classes of pairs of
(S,M)
, where
S
is a nondegenerate oriented quadratic ring and
M
is an equivalence class of balanced rank 3 ideals of
S
. Under this bijection, the discriminant of a senary alternating 3-form is equal to the discriminant of the corresponding quadratic ring.

To construct the senary alternating 3-form corresponding to a given pair
(S,M)
, let
\langle 1,\tau\rangle
be a positively oriented
\mathbb{Z}
-basis of
S
and let
\mathcal{A}=\langle\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3},\alpha_{4},\alpha_{5},\alpha_{6}\rangle
be a positively oriented
\mathbb{Z}
-basis of
M
. Since
M
is balanced, we have

(56)

\mathrm{det}(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j},\alpha_{k})=a^{\prime}_{ijk}+a_{ijk}\tau,

for some integers
a_{ijk}
and
a^{\prime}_{ijk}
satisfying

a_{ijk}=-a_{ikj}=-a_{jik}=-a_{kij},

and

a^{\prime}_{ijk}=-a^{\prime}_{ikj}=-a^{\prime}_{jik}=-a^{\prime}_{kij},

for
i,j,k\in\{1,2,3,4,5,6\}
. Then,

E=\sum_{i,j,k=1}^{6}a_{ijk}e_{i}\wedge e_{j}\wedge e_{k}

is the senary alternating 3-form corresponding to
M
, where
\langle e_{1},e_{2},...,e_{6}\rangle
is the standard basis of
\mathbb{Z}^{6}
. The form

E^{\prime}=\sum_{i,j,k=1}^{6}a^{\prime}_{ijk}e_{i}\wedge e_{j}\wedge e_{k}

is called the
companion form
of
E
. Let
\underline{X},\underline{Y}
and
\underline{Z}
be arbitrary vectors in
M
and let
\underline{x}=(x_{1},x_{2},...,x_{6})^{t},\underline{y}=(y_{1},y_{2},...,y_{6})^{t}
and
\underline{z}=(z_{1},z_{2},...,z_{6})^{t}
be their respective
\mathbb{Z}^{6}
coordinates with respect to the
\mathbb{Z}
-basis
\mathcal{A}
of
M
. Using (
56
), we can write

(57)

\mathrm{det}(\underline{X},\underline{Y},\underline{Z})=E(\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z})\tau+E^{\prime}(\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z}).

Again using results of Bass (
[
1
]
) and Serre (
[
25
]
) we can deduce that any rank 3 ideal
M
of
S
is (isomorphic to) a decomposable ideal i.e. the direct sum of rank 1 ideals, and that any projective balanced rank 3 ideal of
S
is equivalent to
S\oplus S\oplus S
. This implies that the class group of senary alternating
3
-forms of any given discriminant
D
is the trivial group. The identity element of
\text{Cl}(\wedge^{3}\mathbb{Z}^{6};D)
is the class of
E_{{\mathtt{id}},D}
, where

E_{{\mathtt{id}},D}=\wedge_{2,2,2}(A_{{\mathtt{id}},D}).

7.2.
Composition identities for senary alternating 3-forms

Let
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
be two senary alternating
3
-forms in
\wedge^{3}\mathbb{Z}^{6}
of common discriminant
D
. We define their form product
E_{1}\star E_{2}
to be

\displaystyle(E_{1}\star E_{2})((\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z});(\underline{u},\underline{v},\underline{w}))=E_{1}

\displaystyle(\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z})E_{2}^{\prime}(\underline{u},\underline{v},\underline{w})

\displaystyle+E_{1}^{\prime}(\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z})E_{2}(\underline{u},\underline{v},\underline{w})+\epsilon E_{1}(\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z})E_{2}(\underline{u},\underline{v},\underline{w}),

for
\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z},\underline{u},\underline{v},\underline{w}\in\mathbb{Z}^{6}
. We have the following result on composition of senary alternating
3
-forms.

Theorem 7.1
.

Let
E=E_{{\mathtt{id}},D}
be the projective senary alternating
3
-form of discriminant
D
that represents the identity element in
\mathrm{Cl}(\wedge^{3}\mathbb{Z}^{6};D)
and
\epsilon\in\{0,1\}
congruent to
D
mod
4
. For
i=1,2,3
, let

\underline{x_{i}},\underline{y_{i}},\underline{z_{i}},\underline{u_{i}},\underline{v_{i}},\underline{w_{i}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
and let
\underline{x}=(\underline{x_{1}},\underline{x_{2}},\underline{x_{3}}),\underline{y}=(\underline{y_{1}},\underline{y_{2}},\underline{y_{3}}),\underline{z}=(\underline{z_{1}},\underline{z_{2}},\underline{z_{3}}),\underline{u}=(\underline{u_{1}},\underline{u_{2}},\underline{u_{3}}),\underline{v}=(\underline{v_{1}},\underline{v_{2}},\underline{v_{3}}),\underline{w}=(\underline{w_{1}},\underline{w_{2}},\underline{w_{3}}).

Then, we have the composition identity

\displaystyle(E\star E)(\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z};\underline{u},\underline{v},\underline{w})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E\Bigg[\left(\sum_{i=1}^{3}R^{\iota}(\underline{x_{i}},\underline{u_{i}}),\sum_{i=1}^{3}R^{\iota}(\underline{y_{i}},\underline{u_{i}}),\sum_{i=1}^{3}R^{\iota}(\underline{z_{i}},\underline{u_{i}})\right)^{t},

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{i=1}^{3}R^{\iota}(\underline{x_{i}},\underline{v_{i}}),\sum_{i=1}^{3}R^{\iota}(\underline{y_{i}},\underline{v_{i}}),\sum_{i=1}^{3}R^{\iota}(\underline{z_{i}},\underline{v_{i}})\right)^{t},

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{i=1}^{3}R^{\iota}(\underline{x_{i}},\underline{w_{i}}),\sum_{i=1}^{3}R^{\iota}(\underline{y_{i}},\underline{w_{i}}),\sum_{i=1}^{3}R^{\iota}(\underline{z_{i}},\underline{w_{i}})\right)^{t}\Bigg].

where
R
is the cube of discriminant
D
given by

R=\hbox to92.64pt{\vbox to84.81pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.83301pt\lower-6.55522pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{80.40997pt}{17.39726pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

Proof.

The senary alternating
3
-form
E=E_{{\mathtt{id}},D}
corresponds to the pair
(S,S\oplus S\oplus S)
. Indeed, let
\underline{X}=(X_{1},X_{2},X_{3}),\underline{Y}=(Y_{1},Y_{2},Y_{3})
and
\underline{Z}=(Z_{1},Z_{2},Z_{3})
be arbitrary vectors in
S\oplus S\oplus S
.
We fix the
\mathbb{Z}
-basis
\langle 1,\tau\rangle
of
S
. For
i=1,2,3
, let
\underline{x_{i}},\underline{y_{i}},\underline{z_{i}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
be the respective
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
coordinates of
X_{i},Y_{i},Z_{i}
with respect to the fixed
\mathbb{Z}
-basis of
S
. Then, using (
56
), we can write

\mathrm{det}(\underline{X},\underline{Y},\underline{Z})=E(\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z})\tau+E^{\prime}(\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z}),

where
\underline{x}=(\underline{x_{1}},\underline{x_{2}},\underline{x_{3}}),\underline{y}=(\underline{y_{1}},\underline{y_{2}},\underline{y_{3}}),\underline{z}=(\underline{z_{1}},\underline{z_{2}},\underline{z_{3}})
.

Similarly, let
\underline{U}=(U_{1},U_{2},U_{3}),

\underline{V}=(V_{1},V_{2},V_{3})
and
\underline{W}=(W_{1},W_{2},W_{3})
be arbitrary vectors in
S\oplus S\oplus S
and
\underline{u_{i}},\underline{v_{i}},\underline{w_{i}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
be the respective
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
coordinates of
U_{i},V_{i},W_{i}
. Let
\underline{u}=(\underline{u_{1}},\underline{u_{2}},\underline{u_{3}}),\underline{v}=(\underline{v_{1}},\underline{v_{2}},\underline{v_{3}}),\underline{w}=(\underline{w_{1}},\underline{w_{2}},\underline{w_{3}}).
Now, let
\underline{A},\underline{B},\underline{C}\in S\oplus S\oplus S
be defined by the relation

(58)

\begin{bmatrix}X_{1}&X_{2}&X_{3}\\
Y_{1}&Y_{2}&Y_{3}\\
Z_{1}&Z_{2}&Z_{3}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}U_{1}&V_{1}&W_{1}\\
U_{2}&V_{2}&W_{2}\\
U_{3}&V_{3}&W_{3}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}A_{1}&B_{1}&C_{1}\\
A_{2}&B_{2}&C_{2}\\
A_{3}&B_{3}&C_{3}\end{bmatrix}.

Let
\underline{a_{i}},\underline{b_{i}},\underline{c_{i}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
be the respective
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
coordinates of
A_{i},B_{i},C_{i}
and let
\underline{a}=(\underline{a_{1}},\underline{a_{2}},\underline{a_{3}}),\underline{b}=(\underline{b_{1}},\underline{b_{2}},\underline{b_{3}}),\underline{c}=(\underline{c_{1}},\underline{c_{2}},\underline{c_{3}})
. The cube
R
corresponds to the balanced triple
(S,S,S)
where the
\mathbb{Z}
-basis is chosen to be
\langle 1,\tau\rangle
for all the three copies of
S
. We note that for
(x_{1},x_{2})^{t},(u_{1},u_{2})^{t}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
,

\displaystyle(x_{1}+x_{2}\tau)(u_{1}+u_{2}\tau)=a_{1}+a_{2}\tau,

where
(a_{1},a_{2})^{t}=R^{\iota}((x_{1},x_{2})^{t},(u_{1}u_{2})^{t})
.
Using this equation and taking determinants in (
58
) gives us

(59)

\displaystyle[E(\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z})\tau+E^{\prime}(\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z})]\cdot[E(\underline{u},\underline{v},\underline{w})\tau+E^{\prime}(\underline{u},\underline{v},\underline{w})]=E(\underline{a},\underline{b},\underline{c})\tau+E^{\prime}(\underline{a},\underline{b},\underline{c}),

where
\underline{a},\underline{b},\underline{c}
are given by

\begin{split}\underline{a}&=(\underline{a_{1}},\underline{a_{2}},\underline{a_{3}})=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{3}R^{\iota}(\underline{x_{i}},\underline{u_{i}}),\sum_{i=1}^{3}R^{\iota}(\underline{y_{i}},\underline{u_{i}}),\sum_{i=1}^{3}R^{\iota}(\underline{z_{i}},\underline{u_{i}})\right)^{t},\\
\underline{b}&=(\underline{b_{1}},\underline{b_{2}},\underline{b_{3}})=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{3}R^{\iota}(\underline{x_{i}},\underline{v_{i}}),\sum_{i=1}^{3}R^{\iota}(\underline{y_{i}},\underline{v_{i}}),\sum_{i=1}^{3}R^{\iota}(\underline{z_{i}},\underline{v_{i}})\right)^{t},\\
\underline{c}&=(\underline{c_{1}},\underline{c_{2}},\underline{c_{3}})=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{3}R^{\iota}(\underline{x_{i}},\underline{w_{i}}),\sum_{i=1}^{3}R^{\iota}(\underline{y_{i}},\underline{w_{i}}),\sum_{i=1}^{3}R^{\iota}(\underline{z_{i}},\underline{w_{i}})\right)^{t}.\end{split}

Finally, equating the coefficients of
\tau
on both sides of equation (
59
), we get

\displaystyle(E\star E)(\underline{x},\underline{y},\underline{z};\underline{u},\underline{v},\underline{w})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E\Bigg[\left(\sum_{i=1}^{3}R^{\iota}(\underline{x_{i}},\underline{u_{i}}),\sum_{i=1}^{3}R^{\iota}(\underline{y_{i}},\underline{u_{i}}),\sum_{i=1}^{3}R^{\iota}(\underline{z_{i}},\underline{u_{i}})\right)^{t},

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{i=1}^{3}R^{\iota}(\underline{x_{i}},\underline{v_{i}}),\sum_{i=1}^{3}R^{\iota}(\underline{y_{i}},\underline{v_{i}}),\sum_{i=1}^{3}R^{\iota}(\underline{z_{i}},\underline{v_{i}})\right)^{t},

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{i=1}^{3}R^{\iota}(\underline{x_{i}},\underline{w_{i}}),\sum_{i=1}^{3}R^{\iota}(\underline{y_{i}},\underline{w_{i}}),\sum_{i=1}^{3}R^{\iota}(\underline{z_{i}},\underline{w_{i}})\right)^{t}\Bigg].

∎

8.
Conclusion

We identify some possible future directions and generalizations of our work.

(1)

We use the correspondence between forms and ideals in each higher composition law to compute the composition of given forms and to write the explicit composition identities. It would be interesting to find a more direct approach to the composition identities without having to rely on the correspondence with ideals.

(2)

Our results are potentially applicable to questions of representations of integers by homogeneous forms, e.g. binary cubic forms. Similar identities in different settings are applied to such problems in recent works of Choudhry
[
10
]
and Duke
[
14
]
.

(3)

Bhargava investigates further higher composition laws in
[
4
]
,
[
5
]
and
[
7
]
. We expect our methods to extend these settings. More interesting are extensions beyond the case of prehomogeneous vector spaces. A catalog of potential candidates for study appears in the work of Bhargava and Ho
[
2
]
on coregular spaces. The papers
[
10
]
and
[
14
]
cited above (dealing with
3\times 3\times 3
cubes and symmetric
2\times 2\times 2\times 2
hypercubes, respectively) are examples.
